## Page 1


Bordisms and unbounded KK-theory
Robin J. Deeley, Magnus Goffeng, Bram Mesland
arXiv:2603.26450v1  [math.KT]  27 Mar 2026


## Page 2


Abstract. This monograph studies KK-theory in its unbounded model. The
central object is the KK-bordism group obtained by imposing the KK-bordism
relation on unbounded KK-cycles. In the paradigm of noncommutative ge-
ometry, an unbounded KK-cycle is a noncommutative geometry in its own
right and our approach allow for the study of mildly noncommutative geome-
tries (orbifolds, foliations et cetera) as if they were closed manifolds.
The
techniques we introduce enable us to directly import manifold techniques and
arguments into the important yet technical field of unbounded KK-theory.
Recent decades has seen a tremendous progress in the study of the un-
bounded model for KK as well as secondary invariants, the first motivated
by refining computational tools in Kasparov’s KK-theory and the second by
applications to geometry and topology. The aim of this work is to provide a
common framework for these two areas: equipping unbounded KK-cycles with
a geometrically motivated relation recovering Kasparov’s KK-theory that is
computationally tractable for working with secondary invariants.

## Page 5


1. INTRODUCTION
5
1. Introduction
The seminal Atiyah-Singer index theorem [7] dates back to the 1960’s and
exhibits the deep connection between algebra, topology and geometry on the one
hand, and analysis on the other hand that elliptic operators and their index theory
provide. To facilitate the connection further, Atiyah [3] envisioned a (generalized)
homology theory where abstract elliptic operators played the role of cycles and the
index theorem was a functoriality statement. Atiyah’s vision lead Kasparov [101,
102] to form his KK-theory and Baum-Douglas [12, 13] to form their geometric
K-homology. The purpose of this monograph is to provide a new approach that
marries the analytic techniques of Kasparov’s KK-theory with the geometric ideas
underlying Baum-Douglas’ geometric K-homology and lies closer to current trends
in geometry, topology and mathematical physics. Furthermore, this work aims to
provide a coherent set of techniques for unbounded KK-theory that encompasses
existing applications to index theory, K-homology and noncommutative geometry.
Kasparov’s bivariant K-theory (KK-theory) is a fundamental tool in the study
of C∗-algebras and applications of C∗-algebas to topology, geometry and mathemat-
ical physics. Being rooted in C∗-algebra theory, KK-theory is analytic in nature
and its origins lie in the analytic side of the Atiyah-Singer index theorem. Even so
the bounded cycles used to define elements in the KK-group are at first glance far
from the operators typically associated with index theory problems. Such operators
are typically elliptic first order differential operators and in particular unbounded.
Explicit examples are the de Rham operator, the signature operator, the spin or
spinc Dirac operator, among others.
From an opposing point of view, one has
Baum-Douglas’ geometric K-homology that takes spinc-manifolds as its cycles and
closely models the geometric applications. Baum-Douglas’ geometric K-homology
is a K-homological incarnation of classical bordism groups where the Thom iso-
morphism has been implemented by means of having coefficients in K-theory. Kas-
parov’s analytic theory and Baum-Douglas geometric theory both come with their
own virtues that are of importance in applications. Despite the existence of natural
isomorphisms of the theories the discrepancy of the two creates technical problems
and created pockets of ad hoc results that have been reinvented several times.
Based on the abundance of the naturally occurring unbounded examples and
the conflict between geometric and analytic, we look to the work of Baaj-Julg [8]
who defined the notion of unbounded KK-cycle. The bounded transform takes
these unbounded operators to Kasparov’s notion of bounded KK-cycles and Baaj
and Julg proved that it is a surjective map onto the KK-group [8] in favourable
circumstances. The standard examples from index theory listed above as well as
Baum-Douglas’ geometric cycles are easily shown to give Baaj-Julg cycles. Perhaps
more to the point in our context, these examples give canonical Baaj-Julg cycles,
but do not give canonical bounded KK-cycles. The associated bounded KK-cycles
depend on the choice of a cut off function used in the bounded transform. To be
clear, the KK-class associated to such an operator is canonical as different choices
of cut off function lead to homotopic KK-cycles. Through this viewpoint, one can
see the passage from defining KK-theory from bounded cycles to unbounded cycles
as a movement to reduce the choices required to define cycles from index theoretical
inputs and provide quantitative control.
Baaj-Julg’s unbounded approach to KK-theory plays an integral part in Alain
Connes’ program for spectral noncommutative geometry [44, 43, 41, 45], see also


## Page 6


6
CONTENTS
[120, 138]. The unbounded aspects of spectral noncommutative geometry have
found numerous applications in particle physics [32, 36, 37, 122, 130, 155] and
topological states of matter [19, 20, 24, 25, 26, 104]. Unbounded KK-theory
has in the mentioned applications to theoretical physics found utility in its explicit
nature connecting analysis to geometry. In recent years the focus has been on the
constructive approach to the Kasparov product [27, 94, 95, 96, 112, 119, 125,
126], a method we shall only occasionally use in the monograph but it will not
be center stage. When using Kasparov products, the unbounded approach by no
means has a computational monopoly at the level of homotopy classes.
In the
same way, we by no means claim that the unbounded approach we take in this
monograph is a unique method for solving index theoretical problems. However,
the unbounded approach does carry a level of computational ubiquity
that we want to highlight. In this monograph we take unbounded KK-theory
as a framework in which to perform computations in index theory and KK-theory
pivoted towards geometric applications. With the viewpoint on noncommutative
geometry as geometry, the techniques of this monograph provides geometric proofs
for analytically technical results in index theory and bivariant KK-theory.
An equivalence relation is needed so that one can define KK-theory using
Baaj-Julg’s unbounded cycles.
There are various results towards defining KK-
groups via the unbounded picture, [97, 128] define unbounded KK-groups using
a homotopy relation as Kasparov did and [51] defines KK-bordism groups.
In
KK-theory there is an informal mantra that any reasonable equivalence relation
on bounded KK-cycles will lead to the KK-group. The main analytic result
of the present work is a proof that a similar situation occurs in the
context of unbounded cycles. Nevertheless we have concentrated our study on
a relation defined using Hilsum’s notion of bordism. Why have we chosen to do so?
The answer again lies in our desire to make constructions in index theory, if not
canonical, at least as explicit as possible. Hilsum [82, 84, 85] has an extensive
body of work rich in ideas that aligns with the desire for canonical, and
geometric constructions in the realm of noncommutative geometry. The
motivation to search for a geometric as well as explicit equivalence relation is based
on our goal to explore the geometry and index theory arising from the equivalence
relation, much in analogy with Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index theory [4, 5, 6], in order
to place the above listed works on applications of noncommutative geometry in a
coherent foundation.
Some terminology is required to proceed further with the
justification of our choice of Hilsum bordism as the main relation of study.
1.1. Analytic versus geometric models for K-homology. Let us discuss
the different models and how the associated homology theories conceptualize the
Atiyah-Singer index theorem. For the moment we will work with K-homology as
it illustrates the general case of KK-theory. Let X be a finite CW-complex. The
Atiyah-Singer index theorem can be encoded into K-homology by considering two
models. On the topological side one has the Baum-Douglas model for K-homology
[12, 16], which uses cycles of the form (M, E, f) where
(1) M is a closed, smooth, Riemannian, spinc-manifold;
(2) E is a smooth Hermitian vector bundle over M;
(3) f : M →X is a continuous map.


## Page 7


1. INTRODUCTION
7
On the analytic side one has Kasparov’s model, as described in Higson-Roe’s clas-
sical book [74], which uses cycles of the form (H, ρ, F) where
(1) H is a complex separable Hilbert space;
(2) ρ : C(X) →B(H) is a representation;
(3) F ∈B(H) satisfying the following for any a ∈C(X),
ρ(a)(F −F ∗) ∈K(H), ρ(a)(F 2 −I) ∈K(H), [ρ(a), F] ∈K(H)
Kasparov’s analytic cycles form what Atiyah [3] called abstract elliptic operators.
There is a map from geometric K-homology to analytic K-homology that is
defined via
(1)
(M, E, f) 7→[(L2(M; S ⊗E), ρ, β(DE))]
where S is the spinor bundle associated to the spinc-structure, L2(M; S ⊗E) are
the L2-section of S ⊗E, ρ : C(X) →L2(M; S ⊗E) is defined via multiplication of
functions, β(x) := x(1+x2)−1/2 is the bounded transform, and D is a Dirac operator
associated to S⊗E. We note that this map involves a choice of connection to obtain
the Dirac operator, a choice of function in the bounded transform, et cetera and
hence is not quite defined at the level of cycles but rather maps a geometric cycle
to a class in analytic K-homology.
The map (1) induces an isomorphism from
geometric K-homology to analytic K-homology [16, 92, 137] and in particular,
when X is a point we have the Atiyah-Singer’s index theorem as stated in [12, 13]
and discussed in more detail in [17, 18].
Intermediary between the geometric Baum-Douglas model and the analytic
Kasparov model sits Baaj-Julg’s unbounded picture.
Its role in relation to the
geometric and analytic models was not clarified until the works [51, 97, 128],
and we aim to provide further context in this work. The cycles for Baaj-Julg’s
unbounded picture can be motivated by the appearance of Dirac operators and the
bounded transform in the transformation (1). Baaj-Julg’s unbounded cycles have
the form (H, ρ, D) where D is an unbounded self adjoint operator that satisfies
similar condition to F, for details see Definition 2.1 below.
These unbounded
cycles are a variation on Connes’ notion of spectral triples, the key object of study
in Connes’ program for spectral noncommutative geometry [40, 43, 69]. The reader
can find a number of applications and examples in the literature [41, 42, 44, 45,
63, 66, 67, 94, 109, 120, 121, 129, 138, 140].
The unbounded cycles sit between the geometric and analytic realization of
K-homology as we can factor the map (1) as
(2)
(M, E, f) 7→(L2(M; S ⊗E), ρ, DE) 7→[(L2(M; S ⊗E), ρ, β(DE))]
We note that we can by augmenting the (M, E, f) cycles with further data realize
the first map in (2) as a map at the level of cycles. Indeed, following [81, 105],
one can consider cycles of the form (M, E, f, DE) where M and E are as before,
f is assumed to be Lipschitz and DE is a specific Dirac operator associated to the
Clifford bundle S ⊗E.
Based on this discussion at the cycles level, a desirable feature of an equiva-
lence relation on unbounded cycles is that an equivalence between geometric cycles
explicitly produces an equivalence of the associated unbounded cycles. Hilsum’s
notion of bordism has this property, but first we will take a moment to unpack
the previous sentence. The relation on geometric cycles is defined by taking the


## Page 8


8
CONTENTS
relation generated by direct sum/disjoint union, bordism and vector bundle modi-
fication. For example, suppose that (M, E, f, D) is a cycle that is a boundary and
(W, F, g, DW ) is an explicit choice of bordism with boundary (M, E, f, D). The as-
sociated analytic K-homology element is the zero element but we can ask for more;
namely, is there an explicit Hilsum bordism associated to (W, F, g, DW )? Indeed
there is such a Hilsum bordism; this should not be too surprising since Hilsum
defined his notion bordim to naturally include examples from differential geometry.
Somewhat more surprisingly, the two other parts of relation (direct sum/disjoint
and vector bundle modification) from geometric K-homology also lead to explicit
KK-bordisms. In summary, using Hilsum bordism as our relation strengthens the
relationship between the geometric cycles of the form (M, E, f, D) and the analytic
cycles of the form (H, ρ, D).
Strengthening the relationship between geometric and analytic models is not
only satisfying from a philosophical point of view but also has important implica-
tions in geometry and topology. The relative constructions considered by the first
and second listed authors [47, 48, 49, 50, 56, 55] are a prototypical example.
Each of these relative constructions are based on the bordism relation in geometric
K-homology (and bivariant generalizations). Once the geometric cycles and associ-
ated group have been defined one needs a map to the relevant analytic group. At the
KK-theory level the relative group can be obtained from a mapping cone construc-
tion and the associated long exact sequence. The Hilsum bordism relation allows
us to apply the relative process to unbounded analytic cycles in a way completely
analogous to the one used on geometric cycles. In doing so, the methods of this
monograph will serve to unify the various isomorphisms between the geometric and
analytic relative groups considered in [48, 50]. We complete this work in the second
part of this monograph [52]. The motivation for relative constructions is to better
understand secondary invariants; a methodology that has been studied in the con-
text of positive scalar curvature metrics [81, 132, 134, 143], homotopy structure
on manifolds and Lipschitz manifolds [78, 79, 80, 81, 131, 133, 160, 161] and
for foliations [1, 2, 39, 68, 84, 108, 151, 152]. All these examples have one thing
in common, they concern mildly noncommutative geometries constructed from a
group action on a classical geometric object.
1.2. Main results and overview of the monograph. In this monograph
we overview the existing sphere of examples that in the literature has motivated
the usage of KK-theory and NCG, as well as study how Hilsum’s KK-bordisms
fit into this picture. The viewpoint we take is that of unbounded KK-theory and
the reader can find a summary of the basic elements underlying this theory in
Section 2. There is to the authors’ knowledge no current, complete overview of
modern examples in NCG of geometric origins. Part I aims at providing a partial
remedy to this omission. The field is too big to aim for completeness, but Section 3
contains an extensive overview of the topical examples central to this work and to
unbounded KK-theory at large. We claim no novelty but we provide the examples
in order to showcase how various NCG-structures fit into unbounded KK-theory.
Let us summarize the examples of Section 3. We refer the reader to Section 2 for
notations and terminology. There are canonical equivariant unbounded cycles and
chains associated with several geometric situations, such as:
(1) For Riemannian manifolds equipped with a Dirac type operator twisted
by a C∗-bundle, possibly only Lipschitz manifolds, there is an associated


## Page 9


1. INTRODUCTION
9
unbounded chain. If the manifold is complete we arrive at a closed cycle
for Lipschitz functions vanishing at infinity, and there is a rich theory of
Dirac-Schr¨odinger operators.
(2) For any K-oriented map f : X →Y of smooth manifolds, the wrong way
map comes from an unbounded cycle.
(3) The NCG-philosophy extends to truly noncommutative cases producing
cycles and chains also for foliations, Cuntz-Pimsner algebras, groupoid C∗-
algebras with cocycles, continuous trace algebras as well as Fell algebras.
(4) Moreover, in the above cases when there is a group action present we ar-
rive at equivariant cycles and chains that allow for descent constructions
and Baum-Connes assembly, producing mildly noncommutative geome-
tries already from commutative geometries.
We proceed in Section 4 by studying analytic techniques for manipulating con-
tinuity and “smooth” subalgebras. The results of Subsections 4.1 and 4.2 allow
us to deduce automatic continuity of chains and expand smooth subalgebras via
functional calculus, these results are on the whole well known. In Subsection 4.3
we introduce methods of complex interpolation [21] into unbounded KK-theory;
this tool is well studied in functional analysis but in noncommutative geometry it
has at best been hinted at [66].
We study Hilsum’s notion of KK-bordisms in unbounded KK-theory in more
detail in Part II, where we connect KK-bordisms to the examples of Part I. A
significant feature is that KK-bordisms forms an equivalence relation on the un-
bounded KK-cycles for a pair (A, B) of a ∗-algebra A and a C∗-algebra, and the
set of equivalence classes Ω∗(A, B) forms an abelian group under direct sum of cy-
cles. This fact goes back to [51], and by a result of Hilsum the bounded transform
induces a well defined mapping
(3)
β : Ω∗(A, B) →KK∗(A, B),
where A is the C∗-closure of A. When A satisfies that the bounded transform
(3) is an isomorphism for all B, we say that A is Ω∗-admissible. As we
discuss soon in Theorem 4, large classes of ∗-algebras are Ω∗-admissible..
We shall see how new, purely noncommutative, phenomena arises from KK-
bordisms. An important feature is that in all key examples we can construct canoni-
cal KK-bordisms from pure analytic geometric reasons; a situation that differs from
homotopies. The reader can find the precise notions described in Section 5, Section
6 focuses on general structure, while the main examples in Section 7 relate back to
the examples of Section 3. The results in Sections 6 and 7 can be summarized as
follows.
Theorem 1. There are canonical equivariant KK-bordisms associated with
(1) Complete Riemannian manifolds with boundary equipped with a Dirac type
operator twisted by a C∗-bundle, possibly only Lipschitz, see Subsections
7.1, 7.10.
(2) A positive scalar curvature metric on a compact manifold (see Subsec-
tion 7.11) or two homotopic topological manifolds in dimension ̸= 4 (see
Subsection 7.12).
(3) The vector bundle modification relation in geometric K-homology imple-
menting the Thom isomorphism [12, 13], or more generally there is a
null-bordism for weakly degenerate unbounded cycles, see Subsection 6.3.

(4) A homotopy of unbounded operators (Dt)t∈[0,1] induces a bordism with
boundary (E, D0) + (−(E, D1)), see Subsection 6.1.
(5) A reasonable perturbation V of an operator D in an unbounded cycle in-

(6) A homotopy (πt)t∈[0,1] of the left action in an unbounded cycle induces a

(7) Performing order reduction induces a bordism with boundary (E, D) +
(−(E, D|D|−α)) for α ∈[0, 1) and also KK-bordisms can be order reduced
in a similar fashion. See more in Subsection 6.5.
There is also well defined operations for descent of equivariant cycles, that for any
second countable locally compact group G produces a map
⋊G : ΩG
∗(A, B) →Ω∗(A ⋊c G, B ⋊G).
Here A ⋊c G := Cc(G, A) with the twisted convolution product. And finally, for a

µX : ΩG
∗(Lip0(X), B) →Ω∗(C, B ⋊G).
The conceptual benefit of KK-bordisms is not just the canonical KK-bordisms
arising in key examples, but in addition that it allows for the importing of geometric
notions and ideas into the technical area of KK-theory. A concrete example is how
wrong way maps give rise to unbounded cycles that up to explicit KK-bordisms is
independent of geometric choices, see Section 7.5. A more lofty, yet conceptually
interesting, feature is that rigourous proofs by picture are a possibility with KK-
bordisms. We develop this line of thought in Part III. The primary techniques of
straightening the angle, the doubling construction, gluing on infinite cylinders (`a
la Melrose’s b-calculus [124]) are developed in Section 8. We also extend the ideas
of complex interpolation from Subsection 4.3 to KK-bordisms in Subsection 8.5.
These techniques are applied in Section 9 to prove the following rigidity results for
the KK-bordism groups.

restriction mapping induces an isomorphism

1. INTRODUCTION
11
For the trivial group G and under assumptions similar to Ω∗-admissibility, we
show in Section 14 how KK-bordism groups additionally to the rigidity properties
of Theorem 2 enjoys exactness and homotopy invariance.
Index theory has found numerous applications in geometry and topology [1,
2, 39, 47, 48, 49, 50, 55, 56, 78, 79, 80, 81, 131, 132, 133, 134, 143,
151, 152, 160, 161], and for manifolds with boundary the relevant index theory
comes from the work of Atiyah-Patodi-Singer [4, 5, 6] – APS index theory. In
particular higher APS index theory is of importance in topological applications
[71, 83, 90, 117], where the index theory arises from mildly noncommutative
geometries with boundary. Following the general spirit of this work, we show in
Section 10 that KK-bordisms behave much like a noncommutative manifold with
boundary admitting a fully developed higher APS index theory.
Such methods
will play an important role in the follow up work [52] where the above mentioned
canonical geometric KK-bordisms will be utilized for secondary invariants. We
summarize the results of Section 10 in the following theorem.
Theorem 3. Let B be a unital C∗-algebra and X = (X◦, Θ, X∂) a (C, B)-
bordism. Then X, or at least its very full modification (for details see Subsection
10.1), admits a trivializing operator A. For X◦= (N , T) and X∂= (E, D), we
define the APS-realization of X with trivializing operator A to be
TAPS(A) :=T ∗,
with domain
Dom TAPS(A) := {f ∈Dom (T ∗) : f|∂X ∈ker χ[0,∞)(D + A)}.
For more details on the domain, see Subsection 10.2. The APS-realization of X
with trivializing operator A is Fredholm and has a well defined index
indAPS(X, A) ∈K∗(B).
The APS-index of KK-bordisms satisfy the ordinary gluing formulas and spectral
flow formulas (for details see Subsection 10.3).
The results discussed above are somewhat independent of the connection to
Kasparov’s KK-theory implemented by the bounded transform. To fully under-
stand the utility of KK-bordisms, we study the mapping properties of the bounded
transform in Part IV. The main technical result of this part is found in Section 11,
where we show that for A countably generated the bounded transform is an iso-
morphism. Our proof is inspired by work of Kaad [97]. Building on this result, and
the rigidity properties summarized in Theorem 2 above we conclude the following
classes of examples where the bounded transform is an isomorphism.
Theorem 4. The bounded transform
β : Ω∗(A, B) →KK∗(A, B),
is an isomorphism for all C∗-algebras B, that is A is Ω∗-admissible, if it is of the
following form:
(1) A is any countably generated ∗-algebra dense in a C∗-algebra.
(2) A = Lip0(X) and A = C0(X) for a Lipschitz manifold X.
(3) A = C∞
c (G) and A = C∗(G) for an etale groupoid G over an open subset
X of a compact, separable, metrizable space (and smooth functions are
defined relative to an embedding into □× C – the Hilbert cube product a
Cantor set). In particular, it holds if G is of one of the following forms:

We do note that there are examples where the bounded transform β : Ω∗(A, B) →
KK∗(A, B) is neither injective nor surjective, e.g. when A = L1(G) for an amenable
group G. We explore such examples in Section 15 and utilize these results in Section
16 to deduce that the KK-bordism groups in general fails to satisfy excision.

2. Elements of unbounded KK-theory
The main object of study in this work is the class of cycles appearing in the
unbounded model of Kasparov’s KK-theory. The unbounded model was first in-
troduced by Baaj-Julg [8], and has since the played a prominent role in noncom-
mutative geometry and index theory [40, 43, 41, 42, 44, 63, 66, 67, 69, 94,
96, 97, 109, 121, 129, 140]. We start by introducing notation and terminology,
mainly following [51]. These notions will be exemplified in the next section.
We let A denote a C∗-algebra and A ⊆A a dense ∗-subalgebra. The structures
we study depends on the choice of A ⊆A, and it can be thought of as a choice of
smooth structure (see [43]). The prototypical example is obtain from, M, a closed
smooth manifold by taking A to be the continuous complex valued functions on M
denoted by C(M), and A to be the smooth complex valued functions on M denoted
by C∞(M). However, as we will see, one is also interested in the case where A is
as in the previous sentence, but A is the complex valued Lipschitz functions on M
denoted by Lip(M).
For a C∗-algebra B, we use letters such as E and F to denote B-Hilbert C∗-
modules and should we want to emphasize the dependence on B we write EB
and FB et cetera. The C∗-algebra of B-adjointable operators on E is denoted by
End∗
B(E) and the ideal of B-compact operators by KB(E). For more details, see
[114]. When speaking of a left action of A or A on E, we mean a ∗-homomorphism
A →End∗
B(E).
A densely defined operator T from E to F will be written as T : E 99K F . The
graph of T is written as Graph(T) := {(ξ, Tξ) ∈E ⊕F : ξ ∈Dom (T)}. We say
that
• T is semi-regular if T ∗is densely defined;
• T is regular if 1 + T ∗T has dense range;
• T is symmetric if E = F and T ⊆T ∗;
• T is self-adjoint if E = F and T = T ∗.
For more on operators on Hilbert C∗-modules, see [95, 114]. Note that for B = C,
E is a Hilbert space and all semi-regular operators are regular. The domain Dom (T)
carries a B-valued inner product
⟨ξ1, ξ2⟩T := ⟨ξ1, ξ2⟩+ ⟨Tξ1, Tξ2⟩,
called the graph inner product.
It is the inner product that makes the graph
inclusion
Dom (T) ,→Graph(T) ⊆E ⊕F ,
ξ 7→ξ ⊕Tξ,
an isometry where the inner product on the codomain is obtained from the one
on E ⊕F . We say that T is closed if Dom (T) is closed with respect to the norm
defined from this inner product; in this case Dom (T) is a Hilbert C∗-module in its
own right (it is unitarily isomorphic to Graph(T)). If T is a closed operator, T is
regular if and only if the inclusion ι : Dom (T) ,→E is adjointable. In this case,
the adjoint is given by
ι∗: E →Dom T,
ξ 7→(1 + T ∗T)−1/2ξ.
Regularity of T turns out to hold if and only if the sub-module Graph(T) ⊆E ⊕F
is complemented. Indeed, if T is regular, then Graph(T) is the image of the graph

In the next definition, we give the necessary definitions of unbounded KK-
chains, cycles, and the relations and operations thereon. The definition of an even
cycle (or chain) is obtained by including the bracketed data; the definition of odd
cycle (or chain) is obtained by removing them.
Definition 2.1. An unbounded KK-chain over (A, B) is a pair, (E, D) where
(1) E is a (graded) Hilbert (A, B)-bimodule via a ∗-homomorphism π : A →
End∗
B(E);
(2) D is an (odd) regular operator acting as an unbounded operator on E
with
π(A) ⊆Lip(D).
We write X = (E, D). Moreover, we say the following.
• X is symmetric if D is symmetric.
• X is half-closed if D is symmetric with
π(A) ⊆Lip0(D, D∗).
• X is closed if it is half-closed and D is self-adjoint. We typically refer to
closed KK-chains as cycles, or if we want to be overly clear a closed cycle.

• X is called a unital cycle if it is a cycle, and A is unital and acts unitally
on E.
• X is Lipschitz if π(A) ⊆Lip(|D|), where |D| :=
√
D∗D.
An isomorphism α : X1
∼
−→X2 of two (A, B)-chains Xi = (Ei, Di) is a unitary
isomorphism α : E1 →E2 of B-modules, intertwining the A-action and satisfying
that
D2α = αD1.
We emphasize that the identity D2α = αD1 is as unbounded operators, and
the condition αDom(D1) = Dom(D2) is implicit.
Remark 2.2. The way the notions of chains and cycles will be used, is that a
cycle forms a cycle for the KK-bordism group thought of as a bivariant homology
theory. Cycles form a structure analogous to a compact manifold in unbounded
KK, compare to Example 3.2 below. The relations imposed on this will be via
KK-bordisms – a special type of chain introduced by Hilsum [82, 84, 85] that can
be thought of as an “unbounded KK-cycle with boundary which is of product type
at the boundary”. Similar ideas have previously appeared in [51] and using more
operator theoretic homotopy relations in [97, 128].
A prototypical example of a cycle is a spectral triple (A, H , D), a notion in-
troduced by Connes [43]. A spectral triple is an (A, C)-cycle. In the paradigm
of noncommutative geometry, spectral triples are to be thought of as a spectral
representation of a Riemannian manifold with self-adjointness of D encoding com-
pleteness of the Riemannian metric [45]. As we shall see below in Example 3.2,
chains relax these geometric conditions and can be thought of as a generalization
of non-compact manifolds, or more general singular manifolds for that matter.
We nevertheless stress that in general the viewpoint taken in this work is not on
cycles and chains as giving us a noncommutative geometry, i.e. as a generalization
of manifolds, but rather that they form cycles and chains in a noncommutative
generalization of bordism theory. The reader should keep Baum-Douglas’ geometric
model for K-homology in mind, [12, 13], where a spinc-manifold M is thought of
as a cycle for the K-homology of M rather than describing its own geometry. This
conceptual difference will not play any role at the level of technicalities but might
be worthwhile to keep in mind.

Throughout the paper, we shall also use a variety of different techniques. These
include
• Kasparov’s KK-theory in its bounded incarnation, see more in [22, 107,
101, 102].
• Results for regularity and adjoints of sums S+T, see more in [51, 95, 119].
• Functional calculus of self-adjoint regular operators on Hilbert C∗-modules,
see more in [114].
• Smooth and holomorphic calculus on local C∗-algebras, see more in [23,
69].
• Complex interpolation of Banach algebras, see more in [21].

3. Examples of cycles and chains
In order to provide further intuition to the cycles, chains and noncommutative
geometries that are of relevance for this work let us collect some salient examples
from the literature before entering the bulk of the text. We also provide examples
of how to construct new cycles and chains from old ones by means of auxiliary
structures. The reader well acquainted with the literature can skip this chapter
on their first read.
For more details and sources of possible KK-bordisms, see
[51, 84, 83, 82]. In this work we treat these noncommutative objects as interesting
in their own right, for further justifications and applications, see [43]. The reader
need not be an expert in all of the examples discussed in this section but can benefit
from revisiting [51] and become familiar with the material in Subsection 3.1 and
3.2.
3.1. Suspension operations on chains. We start by describing a general
procedure for suspending a chain with the Dirac operator on an interval. Suspension
by intervals is a standard technique for manifolds (with boundary) and is not novel
in the realm of unbounded KK-theory, yet somewhat unexplored in the literature.
Most of the technical conclusions in this subsection are derived from [51, Theorem
1.18]. The usage of suspension by intervals is central in the work of Hilsum [85]:
both in defining KK-bordisms and in its relation to KK-theory. Suspensions will
be an important tool throughout this monograph and we now give a number of
variations on this theme.

3.2. Dirac operators on C∗-bundles. An important construction of KK-
chains comes from geometric K-homology [12, 13]. The construction is that of un-
bounded KK-cycles from Dirac operators twisted by C∗-bundles and was explicitly
considered in this context in [51, Section 1.5 and 4], yet is present in a substantial
amount of literature, e.g. [72, 82, 84, 85, 117, 118, 131, 132, 133, 157]. In this
setting, the adjectives closed, half-closed and symmetric all have geometric mean-
ing. Let W denote a complete Riemannian manifold with boundary. Whenever
referring to a manifold, we tacitly mean a smooth manifold. We will use the con-
vention that C∞
c (W ◦) denotes the space of smooth functions compactly supported

3.3. Group actions on cycles and chains. Let us now consider a fairly
general construction relating to the descent of a certain class of equivariant un-
bounded cycles. There is no generally accepted notion of equivariant unbounded
cycles, and a standard reference is [111].
We shall fix a notion for equivariant
unbounded cycles that is sufficient to study proper group actions on manifolds (cf.
Subsection 3.4 below).
Definition 3.11. Consider a second countable locally compact group G acting
on C∗-algebras A and B, and the action of G preserves the dense ∗-subalgebra
A ⊆A. We shall say that (E, D) is a G-equivariant (A, B)-chain if
(1) (E, D) is an (A, B)-chain, E is a G-equivariant B-Hilbert C∗-module and
the left action A →End∗
B(E) is G-equviariant;
(2) any g ∈G preserves Dom (D);
(3) for any g ∈G and a ∈A, a(D −gDg−1) and (D −gDg−1)a extend to
B-linear adjointable operators on E;
(4) for any a ∈A, the functions
g 7→a(D −gDg−1)
and
g 7→(D −gDg−1)a,
are ∗-strongly continuous functions G →End∗
B(E).
The reader can find that our definition of a G-equivariant (A, B)-chain in Def-
inition 3.11 coincides with that of uniformly equivariant unbounded cycle in the
sense of [123, Definition I.2.7], see also [110, Definition 8.7].
The descent construction will be a construction of a (Cc(G, A), B ⋊G)-chain
from a G-equivariant (A, B)-chain.

KK-theory have been studied in detail in the last years [98, 99, 100]. The wrong
way map associated with a K-oriented smooth map f : X →Y of smooth mani-
folds is a class [f!] ∈KK∗(C0(X), C0(Y )). Recall that a K-orientation of a smooth
map f is a choice of spinc-structure on Tf = T ∗X ⊕f ∗T ∗Y . A direct way to con-
struct [f!] is to use the spinc-structure on Tf to construct a Bott like element in
K∗
X(T ∗X × Y ) and apply Poincar´e duality K∗
X(T ∗X × Y ) →KK∗(C0(X), C0(Y )).
Wrong way maps were proven in [39] to be functorial in the sense that if f : X →Y
and g : Y →Z are K-oriented, then
(15)
[(g ◦f)!] = [f!] ⊗C0(Y ) [g!].
We can describe the class [f!] via an unbounded cycle: when returning to this
construction below in Subsection 7.5, we shall see the unbounded cycle is canonical
up to KK-bordism and for an appropriate unbounded Kasparov product fulfills
(15) up to bordism,
Consider a K-oriented smooth map f : X →Y . We can always factor f as
(16)
f = π ◦ι ◦0,
where
i) 0 : X →N is the zero section of a spinc-vector bundle N →X of even
rank;
ii) ι : N →Z is an inclusion as an open subset into a smooth manifold Z;
iii) π : Z →Y is a fibre bundle with compact fibres and a fibrewise spinc-
structure.
Indeed, the maps are constructed as follows. We can choose an embedding j : X →
Z0 into a compact spinc-manifold Z0. Set Z := Z0 × Y and take N as the normal
bundle of the embedding j ×f : X →Z. Without loss of generality, we can assume
that N has even rank. The inclusion ι : N →Z is as a tubular neighborhood of
im(j × f). Finally, π : Z →Y is defined as the projection onto Y . By functoriality
of wrong way maps, we have that
[f!] = [0!] ⊗C0(N) [ι!] ⊗C0(Z) [π!].
We approach the construction of an unbounded representative of [f!] via the factor-
ization (16), reducing the construction to maps from the cases i-iii and to forming

an unbounded Kasparov product.
3.6.1. Case i: wrong way maps from zero sections. Consider an even rank
spinc-vector bundle p : N →X, and as above 0 : X →N will denote the
zero section viewed as a smooth K-oriented map of manifolds. The class [0!] ∈
KK0(C0(X), C0(N)) is the well studied Thom isomorphism associated with the
spinc-structure on N [33, 39, 58]. We fix a metric on the vector bundle N →X.
Let SN →X denote the complex spinor bundle for defining the spinc-structure of
N, i.e. S is a choice of Clifford bundle for N defining a graded irreducible bundle of
modules for the Clifford algebra bundle Cℓ(N) →X. In particular, by direct com-
parison to the constructions in [33, 39], the class [0!] ∈KK0(C0(X), C0(N)) has
an unbounded representative given by the (C0(X), C0(N))-cycle (C0(N, p∗SN), c)
where c is the self-adjoint and regular unbounded multiplier of C0(N, p∗Cℓ(N))
defined from Clifford multiplication by the fibrewise coordinate. In other words, c
is defined from its action on the core Cc(N, p∗Cℓ(N)) by [cξ](x, v) := v.ξ(x, v) for
ξ ∈Cc(N, p∗Cℓ(N)) and where the . denotes Clifford multiplication.
The (C0(X), C0(N))-cycle (C0(N, p∗SN), c) depends only on the choice of met-
ric on N and the choice of complex spinor bundle SN. But in fact, a change of metric
on N or of complex spinor bundle SN (as long as it defines the same spinc-structure)
does not change the unitary equivalence class of (C0(N, p∗SN), c). However, the
choices of spinc-structures on N form an H2(X, Z)-torsor and so does the set of
unitary equivalence classes of possible unbounded representatives (C0(N, p∗SN), c)
as SN varries over possible spinc-structures.
3.6.2. Case ii: wrong way maps from open inclusions. If ι : N →Z is an in-
clusion as an open subset into a smooth manifold Z, the corresponding wrong way
map [ι!] is well known to be represented by the unbounded (C0(N), C0(Z))-cycle
(C0(N)C0(Z), 0) where C0(N)C0(Z) denotes the space C0(N) is viewed as a right
C0(Z)-Hilbert C∗-module via the ideal inclusion C0(N) ,→C0(Z) induced by ι.
For future reference, we remark that despite the unbounded cycle representing [ι!]
is easy to describe it is precisely this factor in f = π◦ι◦0 that creates most analytic
problems since ι(N) ⊆Z is in general not a complete submanifold and as such is
delicate to treat in unbounded KK-theory [99, 126].

3.7. Groupoid quasi-cocycles. We have already seen a variety of cycles on
groupoids above, such as transformation groupoids in Subsection 3.4 or holonomy
groupoids of foliations in Subsection 3.5, and we shall see more of them below in
Subsection 3.9 on Fell algebras. Let us consider a slightly more exotic construction,
extending constructions in [26, 67, 127]. Let G denote a topological groupoid with
object space X.
Definition 3.23. Consider a continuous field of real Hilbert spaces H over the
unit space of G, with a strongly continuous unitary G-action. Let c : G →H be a
continuous mapping such that c(g) ∈Hr(g).
• We say that c is a cocycle if for any composable g, h ∈G we have that
c(gh) = c(g) + gc(h).
If c is a cocycle, we define its kernel ker(c) ⊆G as the closed subgroupoid
{g ∈G : c(g) = 0 ∈Hr(g)}.
• We say that c is a quasi-cocycle if for any g ∈G, the continuous function
r−1(G) ∋h 7→c(gh) −gc(h) ∈Hr(g),
is norm bounded.
• We say that c is proper if for any compact K ⊆X and any bounded set
B ⊆H|K, the set c−1(B) ⊆G is pre-compact.


## Page 32


32
Remark 3.24. We note that there is a one-to-one correspondence between
cocycles and affine isometric actions. To see this, suppose that H is a continuous
field of Hilbert spaces with a strongly continuous unitary G-action. Then a cocycle
c and an affine isometric action πc mutually determine each other from the rule
that c(g) = πc(g).0.
Remark 3.25. A groupoid is said to be a-T-menable if it admits a proper
cocycle on a continuous field of Hilbert spaces H. This notion was used in [152].
In fact, by [152] any amenable groupoid admits a proper cocycle on a continuous
field of Hilbert spaces H. A priori, H is only a field of real Hilbert spaces.
Let us mention a few examples:
(1) If H = X × H, for a Hilbert space H with the trivial action, a cocycle
is just a groupoid cocycle G →H. Unbounded Kasparov cycles for this
context was considered for H = R in [127] and for a finite-dimensional H
in [26].
(2) If G = Γ is a discrete group, H is a Γ-Hilbert space and a (quasi-)cocycle
is simply a group (quasi-)cocycle c : Γ →H. Unbounded Kasparov cycles
were studied in this context in [67]. By definition, a group admits a proper
cocycle if and only if it is a-T-menable (e.g. if it is amenable) and a proper
quasi-cocycle if and only if it is a-TT-menable (e.g. if it is hyperbolic).
Only virtually abelian groups admit proper quasi-cocycles with values in
a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, so in general it is required that H is
infinite-dimensional.
Assume that SH is a continuous field of complex Hilbert spaces over the unit
space of G, with a strongly continuous unitary G-action, and that there is a G-

• c(ξ) ∈SH,x for ξ ∈Hx;
• c(ξ)∗= c(ξ) and c(ξ)2 = ∥ξ∥2;
• for any continuous section ξ of H, c is a strictly continuous section.
Let X denote the unit space of G. Let B denote the C∗-subalgebra of the multiplier
algebra of the norm continuous sections of K(SH) generated by the set
{c(ξ) : ξ a continuous global section of H vanishing at ∞}.

continuous compact supported functions f : G →B such that f(g) ∈Br(g) in the

3.8. Continuous trace algebras. Continuous trace algebras are mildly non-
commutative C∗-algebras. A continuous trace algebra is a C∗-algebra A with Haus-
dorff spectrum such that for any representation π there is a neighborhood U of
[π] ∈ˆA and an element x ∈A such that π′(x) is a rank one projection for all
[π′] ∈U. A continuous trace algebra always arise as the space of global sections
of a bundle of compact operators on some Hilbert space over the spectrum and is
up to stable isomorphism characterized by its spectrum and its Dixmier-Douady
invariant, an element in the third integral ˇCech cohomology of the spectrum. There
are several techniques to treat bivariant K-theory of continuous trace algebras. We
shall focus on a simple example arising from T-duality. In general T-duality pro-
duces a dual continuous trace algebra to a continuous trace algebra whose spectrum
is a principal circle bundle, for a detailed overview see [142].
Consider a principal circle bundle π : Z →X where we assume Z to be a closed
manifold. The U(1)-action on Z induces an R-action and we consider the algebra
A := C(Z) ⋊R. We write A := S(R, C∞(Z)) ⊆A for the dense ∗-subalgebra
of smooth Schwarz functions. We can identify C∞(Z) ∼= ⊕S

3.9. Fell algebras. Fell algebras are one step further than continuous trace
algebras away from commutative spaces. A Fell algebra is a C∗-algebra A such that
for any representation π there is a neighborhood U of [π] ∈ˆA and an element x ∈A
such that π′(x) is a rank one projection for all [π′] ∈U. The definition of a Fell
algebra is formally the same as that of a continuous trace algebra with the Hausdoff
condition on ˆA dropped. Being the spectrum of a C∗-algebra, the spectrum is only
mildly non-Hausdorff, it is always locally locally compact and locally Hausdorff (see
[38, 91]). As in the case of continuous trace algebras, Fell algebras are characterized
up to stable isomorphism by their spectrum and a Dixmier-Duoady type invariant
(see [38, 91]). The seemingly innocent transition from Hausdorff spectrum to non-
Hausdorff spectrum opens up a quite much larger class of examples, see for instance
[54]. An instance where Fell algebras have played an important role in index theory
is in [103] where they were used to describe the γ-element for a group acting on a
building, e.g. a discrete subgroup of a linear adelic group.
Let us consider a simple example of a Fell algebra. Let X be a compact locally
Hausdorff space admitting a local homeomorphism ψ : M →X from a smooth
manifold M. Existence of ψ ensures that X is a smooth compact non-Hausdorff
manifold. The equivalence relation R(ψ) := {(x, y) ∈M × M : ψ(x) = ψ(y)} is
an ´etale groupoid over M, and Aψ := C∗(R(ψ)) is a Fell algebra with spectrum X
(see [38]). We also set Aψ := C∞
c (R(ψ)) viewed as a dense ∗-subalgbra of Aψ.
We assume that M admits an R(ψ)-equivariant Riemannian metric and that
S →M is an R(ψ)-equivariant Clifford bundle. The reader should note that these
choices correspond to picking a Riemannian metric and a Clifford bundle on X.
Riemannian metrics need not exist on non-Hausdorff manifolds (see for example
[103]). However, if a Riemannian metric exists then Clifford bundles can always
be constructed. From this data, and a choice of connection, we can construct a
Dirac operator /DS whose principal symbol is R(ψ)-equivariant that extends to a
self-adjoint operator DS in L2(M, S).
We arrive at a cycle (L2(M, S), DS) for
(Aψ, C).

3.10. Cuntz-Krieger algebras. Cuntz-Krieger algebras form a well studied
class of C∗-algebras. They are often purely infinite making their noncommutative
geometry challenging. In [63], a class of spectral triples on Cuntz-Krieger algebras
was constructed exhausting their K-homology groups. The starting point for this
construction was an unbounded Kasparov cycle over a maximal abelian subalge-
bras, and the spectral triples came out from localizing in points of the spectrum of
the maximal abelian subalgebra. This idea was later extended to Cuntz-Pimsner
algebras in [65] and to the stable Ruelle algebra of a Wieler solenoid in [53]. We
consider a special case of the construction for Cuntz-Pimsner algebras in Example
3.11. For the sake of exemplifying cycles we explain the construction of spectral
triples from [63, 65] in the special case of the Cuntz algebra ON, with a particular
focus on homotopy relations. The spectral triples considered here were in a mildly
modified form studied in a more geometric context in [64] for ON and later in
general in [62].
We now consider a noncommutative example of cycles on the C∗-algebra ON.
This is the universal C∗-algebra generated by N isometries with orthogonal ranges.

3.13. Spin-Dirac operators and positive scalar curvature. Turning to
a more specific example, let W be an n-dimensional spin manifold with boundary
equipped with a Riemannian metric g of positive scalar curvature.
The scalar
curvature sg is a scalar function formally defined as the full contraction of the
metric tensor, and a direct relation to the geometry can be seen from the Taylor
expansion at r = 0 of the Riemannian volume of a ball centered at x ∈W ◦with
small enough radius r > 0:
vol(B(x, r)) = ωnrn

operator defined from the Riemannian metric. For more details, see [116, 132,
143, 134, 144, 160].
The differential operator /DW acts on C∞(W, SW ).
By

/D
2

4 ,
where ∇is the spin connection and ∇† its formal adjoint.
If sg > 0 we say that g has positive scalar curvature and if infx∈W sg(x) > 0
we say that g has uniformly positive scalar curvature. We tacitly assume sg > 0
for the remainder of this example. It is here of interest to note some special cases.
(1) If W is a complete Riemannian manifold, then /DW is essentially self-
adjoint and extends to a self-adjoint operator DW on L2(W, SW ). If g has
uniformly positive scalar curvature the Schr¨odinger-Lichnerowicz formula
shows that DW has a spectral gap at 0 of size infx∈W sg(x). This occurs
for instance if W is the total space of a spin-Galois covering, and g is the
equivariant lift of a metric of positive scalar curvature.
(2) If W is a complete Riemannian manifold with has uniformly positive scalar
curvature, and EB →W is a hermitean flat B-bundle (of bounded geom-
etry) the twisted spin-Dirac operator /DW,E is essentially self-adjoint and
its closure DW,E to a regular operator on L2(W, SW ⊗EB) (see [72]).
Moreover, the Schr¨odinger-Lichnerowicz formula gives the equality
D2
W = ∇∗
E∇E + sg
4 ,
where ∇E is the spin connection twisted by E. In particular, DW,E as a
self-adjoint operator on L2(W, SW ⊗EB) has a spectral gap at 0 of size
infx∈W sg(x).
We shall revisit this class of examples below in Section 7.11 and the follow up
work [52]. The fact that spin-Dirac operators have spectral gaps is an analytic fact
allowing us to construct explicit null-bordisms. We note that in the special cases
considered above we have not mentioned the case of when W has a boundary; in
this case suitable boundary conditions are required and we shall take more care
with such realizations below in Section 7.11 .

3.14. Lipschitz manifolds. An interesting class of geometric examples comes
from Lipschitz manifolds. What makes this class particularly interesting is a theo-
rem of Sullivan [146] stating that any topological manifold in dimension ̸= 4 admits
a Lipschitz structure. As such, there are subtle rigidity questions at hand that we
will study using KK-bordisms. These questions have previously been studied in
for instance [86, 87, 147, 149, 150, 161].
We shall recall some basic notions to set suitable notations and terminology.
Our convention throughout this work is that a manifold is tacitly assumed to be
smooth, and weaker notions of manifolds are singled out with a further adjective.
Recall that a topological manifold M is a Hausdorff space equipped with an atlas
(Uj, ψj)j∈I consisting of an open cover (Uj)j∈I and homeomorphisms ψj : Uj →
Vj ⊆Rnj, where Vj is open.
A topological manifold with boundary is defined
from an atlas with boundary: a collection (Uj, ψj)j∈I as before but the codomain
of the homeomorphism ψj : Uj →Vj ⊆Rnj
+ is an open subset Vj ⊆Rnj
+
:=
{(x1, . . . , xnj) ∈Rnj : xnj ≥0}. For a topological manifold with boundary, the
boundary is denoted by ∂M and has an atlas defined from (Uj ∩ψ−1
j (Vj ∩(Rnj−1 ×
{0})), ψj|)j∈I.
If M is connected, then nj is independent of j. In general, the dimension is
locally constant (with value nj on Uj). We note that it implicitly follows that for
each i, j ∈I, the map
(21)
ψi ◦ψ−1
j
: ψj(Ui ∩Uj) →ψi(Ui ∩Uj),
is a continuous map of open subsets of Rnj (or Rnj
+ if the boundary is non-empty).
Of course, if each of the maps in Equation (21) is smooth we call (Uj, ψj)j∈I a
smooth atlas and we call M a manifold.
If (Uj, ψj)j∈I is an atlas such that the maps in Equation (21) are Lipschitz
continuous, we call (Uj, ψj)j∈I a Lipschitz atlas and we say that M has a Lipschitz
structure.
A topological manifold equipped with a Lipschitz structure is called
a Lipschitz manifold.
A Lipschitz manifold M with Lipschitz atlas (Uj, ψj)j∈I
satisfying that the Jacobian determinants of the Lipschitz mappings appearing in
(21) are a.e. positive, we say that M is an oriented Lipschitz manifold. For more
details, see for instance [86, 150].
Similarly, a Lipschitz manifold with boundary is defined and the boundary of
a Lipschitz manifold with boundary is again a Lipschitz manifold. We note that
if W is a Lipschitz manifold with boundary, then its double 2W := W ∪∂W W is
a Lipschitz manifold. If W is an oriented Lipschitz manifold with boundary, we
orient 2W as W ∪∂W (−W).
Due to its central importance, we state Sullivan’s theorem on Lipschitz struc-
tures on topological manifolds from [146]. The statement in [146] is for topological
manifolds but it follows from the doubling construction that it extends to topolog-
ical manifolds with boundary.
Theorem 3.30. Let M be a topological manifold with boundary and no con-
nected component of dimension 4. Then M can be equipped with the structure of a
Lipschitz manifold with boundary and it is unique up to topological isotopy.
Let us define the relevant geometric structures on Lipschitz manifolds. Since
the transition functions in a Lipschitz atlas are only required to be Lipschitz, it is
in general not the case that the tangent bundle exists as a vector bundle. However,
the tangent bundle and its exterior powers exist as measurable vector bundles so

Definition 3.31. Let W be an oriented Riemannian Lipschitz manifold of
dimension n with Riemannian metric g.
If n is even, we define Dsign as the closure in L2(W, ∧∗) of the restriction
of /DHdR to Lipc(W ◦)Dom( /DHdR). We consider Dsign as an odd operator in the
grading defined from τg.
If n is odd, we define Dsign as the closure in L2(W, ∧+) of the restriction of
/DHdR to Lipc(W ◦)Dom( /DHdR) ∩L2(W, ∧+), where L2(W, ∧+) is as in Equation
(22).
We shall need some further terminology. We say that a Riemannian metric on a
Lipschitz manifold W with boundary is complete if the distance function associated
with the Riemannian metric makes W into a complete metric space.
Proposition 3.32. Let W be an oriented Lipschitz manifold with boundary
equipped with a complete Riemannian metric.
(1) Dsign is symmetric. Moreover, (1+D∗
signDsign)−1 is a C0(W)-locally com-

operator Dsign is self-adjoint if ∂W = ∅.
(2) Any a ∈Lip0(W) preserves Dom (Dsign) and [Dsign, a] extends to a bounded
operator on L2(W, ∧∗W) (or L2(W, ∧+) if the dimension is odd).
(3) Any j ∈Lip0(W ◦) satisfies jDom ((Dsign)∗) ⊆Dom (Dsign).

odd) is closed if ∂W = ∅.
Proof. It follows from the construction that Lipc(W ◦)Dom(Dsign) is a core
for Dsign. Stokes theorem now implies that that Dsign is symmetric on a core, and
so it is symmetric. It was proven in [87] that if a Riemannian metric is complete
and ∂W = ∅, then Dsign is self-adjoint. To complete the proof of item 1), it suffices

Now we turn to twists of the signature operator on Lipschitz manifolds by
hermitean B-bundles, where B is a unital C∗-algebra. As a starting point, we are
using the constructions of Hilsum [88]. A B-bundle EB →W is a locally trivial
bundle of finitely generated projective B-modules. By standard results, EB has a
Lipschitz structure unique up to isomorphism. A hermitean B-bundle is a B-bundle
equipped with a fibrewise B-valued inner product such that if U ⊆W is any open
subset then
⟨ξ1, ξ2⟩E ∈Lip0(U),
for all ξ1, ξ2 ∈Lip0(U, EB).
A Lipschitz connection on EB is a B-linear map ∇: Lip(W, EB) →L∞(W; T ∗W ⊗
EB) such that in any local trivialization EB|U ∼= U × pBN (for some N and some

4. Analysis of chains
The main object of study in this monograph are the cycles and chains of a
pair (A, B). In this subsection we collect some more subtle points concerning the
analysis of these objects. These include automatic continuity results when A is a
Banach algebra, various versions of functional calculus preserving classes of chains
as well as results introducing methods of complex interpolation in the study of
unbounded chains.
4.1. Automatic continuity. For chains where A is a Banach algebra, we can
from the closed graph theorem conclude an automatic continuity result ensuring
“differentiability” of elements from A.
Proposition 4.1 (Automatic continuity of chains). Let B be a C∗-algebra and
A be equipped with a Banach algebra norm ∥· ∥A making the inclusion A ⊆A
continuous. If (E, D) is an (A, B)-chain, then there is a constant C > 0 such that
∥[D, a]∥EndB(E) ≤C∥a∥A,
∀a ∈A.
Proof. Consider the linear mapping [D, −] : A →EndB(E), a 7→[D, a]. This
linear mapping is closed since if aj →a in A and [D, aj] →T in End∗
B(E), then
aj →a in End∗
B(E) (since A ⊆A continuous), so Tξ = [D, a]ξ for all ξ ∈Dom (D),
and it follows that T = [D, a]. The closed graph theorem implies that [D, −] is
bounded and the proposition follows.
□

is not new, see [23, 69], however we present it in a way adapted for our purposes.

4.3. Order reduction and complex interpolation. Consider a Banach ∗-
algebra A densely and continuously embedded in a C∗-algebra A. For α ∈[0, 1],
we write
Aα := [A, A]α,
for the complex interpolation space, which again is a Banach ∗-algebra. For details,
see [21]. Note that A0 = A and A1 = A. We have that A ⊆Aα is dense for all
α ∈[0, 1], hence also Aα ⊆A is dense for all α ∈[0, 1]. The next theorem gives
a procedure for “reducing the order” of a cycle in such a way that it extends to a
complex interpolation space. This result will be used later in this monograph to
study KK-bordism groups of low regularity functions on manifolds, e.g. the algebra
of Lipschitz functions. For a closed and regular operator D we use the notation
FD := D(1 + D∗D)−1/2,
which is an adjointable operator with F ∗
D = FD∗. If D is self-adjoint, then FD is
self-adjoint.

(A, B) with D self-adjoint. Define the operator
Dα := D(1 + D2)(α−1)/2 = FD(1 + D2)α/2,
Dom (Dα) := Dom (1 + D2)α/2 = (1 + D2)−α/2E.
Then the following holds:
(1) Dα is closed, self-adjoint and regular.
(2) (E, Dα) is a symmetric chain for (Aβ, B) with Aβ ⊆Lip(|Dα|) for any
β ∈(α, 1]
(3) If (E, D) is a closed chain for (A, B), then (E, Dα) is a closed chain for
(Aα, B) for any β ∈(α, 1].

## Page 52


5. Hilsum’s notion of bordism and the KK-bordism group
In this section we recall Hilsum’s notion of KK-bordisms of unbounded KK-
cycles [85] and the associated bivariant K-theory group [51]. The geometric un-
derpining and motivation for KK-bordisms go back to Hilsum [82, 84, 85] and
we encourage the reader to read these papers for further context and ideas. While
the material in this section can be found in [51], the results and notations set the
stage for the examples of KK-bordisms we discuss below in Sections 6 and 7. The
KK-bordism group recalled in this section from [51] is the central object of study
in the present work.
As above, we fix C∗-algebras A and B and a dense ∗-subalgebra A ⊆A.
Definition 5.1. A symmetric chain with boundary is a collection X = (Z, Θ, Y)
where
(1) The boundary cycle Y = (E, D) is an odd/even (A, B)-cycle;
(2) The interior chain Z = (N , T) is a symmetric even/odd (A, B)-chain;
(3) The boundary data Θ = (θ, p), describes boundary compatibility in the
sense that
• p is a projection in End∗
B(N ) that commutes with the A-action (p is
even in the graded situation);
• θ : pN →L2[0, 1]ˆ⊠E is an isomorphism, respecting the grading when
there is one (i.e. Z is even and Y is odd).
These objects are compatible in the following sense described using the representa-
tion
b : C([0, 1], A) →End∗
B(N ),
b(ϕ) := ϕ(1)(1 −p) + pθ−1ϕθ.
We require that the following conditions to hold:
a) For ϕ ∈C∞
c (0, 1],
b(ϕ)Dom T ∗⊆Dom T
and
T ∗b(ϕ) = Tb(ϕ)
on
Dom T ∗.
b) For ϕ ∈C∞
c (0, 1),
ϕDom Ψ(D) = θb(ϕ)Dom T
and
T = θ−1Ψ(D)θ
on
b(ϕ)Dom T.
c) For ϕ1, ϕ2 ∈C∞[0, 1] satisfying ϕ1ϕ2 = 0,
b(ϕ1)Tb(ϕ2) = 0.
If additionally Z is a half-closed (C∞
c ((0, 1], A), B)-chain under b 1 we say that
X is a bordism. In this case we say that the (A, B)-cycle ∂X := Y is the boundary
of X and that the half-closed (C∞
c ((0, 1], A), B)-chain X◦:= Z is the interior of X.
We say that X is a filling bordism for its boundary ∂X.
Remark 5.2. In a symmetric chain with boundary X = (Z, Θ, Y), we require
the parity of Z to be different from the parity of Y. Geometrically, it means the
dimension of a boundary drops by one. In terms of cycles, the parity shift also arise
from the boundary data Θ implementing a “local” equivalence of a suspension of
Y with Z “near its boundary”.

Hilsum [85, Section 10] has shown that one can glue two KK-bordisms with
common boundary to produce an honest unbounded KK-cycle. A crucial point
in proving that KK-bordism is an equivalence relation is to show that one can
glue KK-bordisms along common summands in their boundary and obtain a new
KK-bordism, see [51, Section 2.4]. We will need this construction, and review it
shortly here.

10].

More specifically, we need [51, Theorem 2.20], which briefly
put says the following: suppose X = (X◦, Θ, X∂) and X′ = (X′◦, Θ′, X′∂) are two
KK-bordisms, and
X∂= Y1 + Y2
and

bordism X′′ such that
∂X′′ = Y1 + Y3.
A picture describing this setup is found in Figure 3. Of course, the statement “ex-
plicitly constructed” is informal; the reader can see the statement of [51, Theorem
2.20] for the precise details of the construction. The idea is to glue together X◦
with X′◦along the tubular neighborhood (defined from the boundary data) of the
component of the boundary corresponding to Y2. As in Equation (25), we denote

Figure 3. Gluing of two KK-bordisms X and X′ along a common

Proposition 5.10. Let Y1, Y2 and Y3 be (A, B)-cycles and X and X′ be
(A, B)-bordisms. If Y1 ∼bor Y2 via X, i.e. ∂X = Y1 + (−Y2), and Y2 ∼bor Y3
via X′, i.e. ∂X′ = Y2 + (−Y3), then Y1 ∼bor Y3 via X#Y2X′.
Let us now define the bordism relation on the set of isomorphism classes of
(A, B)-cycles.
Definition 5.11. Two (A, B)-cycles Y1 and Y2 are bordant if there exists a
KK-bordism X over (A, B), with ∂X = Y1 + (−Y2).
It was proven in [51, Proposition 2.22] that KK-bordism is an equivalence
relation. Let us sketch why this is the case in terms of results stated below in this
monograph:
• (Transitive) Follows from the gluing construction above in Proposition
5.10.
• (Reflexive) For any cycle Y, the cylinder construction Ψ(Y) (see Definition
3.1) defines a bordism Y ∼bor Y.
• (Symmetric) If Y1 ∼bor Y2 via X, so ∂X = Y1 + (−Y2), then ∂(−X) =
(−Y1) + Y2 and Y2 ∼bor Y1.
Definition 5.12. (See [51, Definition 2.23]) We define the Z/2Z-graded abelian
group Ω∗(A, B) as the set of bordism classes of (A, B)-cycles with addition defined
from the direct sum + of cycles.
It is not immediate that Ω∗(A, B) is a well-defined abelian group. This fact is
proven in [51, Theorem 2.24]. To summarize this argument, it is clear that direct
sum defines the structure of a Z/2Z-graded semigroup on Ω∗(A, B) and since the
bordism relation is reflexive, it follows that for any cycle Y there is a bordism
Y + (−Y) ∼bor 0 so any element admits an inverse. The explicit additive inverse
is given at the level of cycles by −[Y] = [−Y], where [·] denotes the KK-bordism
class.
Remark 5.13. The KK-bordism group Ω∗(A, B) relates to Kasparov’s KK-
group KK∗(A, B) via the bounded transform β : (E, D) 7→(E, D(1 + D2)−1/2).
For more details, see below in Subsection 9.2. In several instances, the bounded


## Page 56


56
transform induces an isomorphism Ω∗(A, B) ∼= KK∗(A, B) as we will show in Part
IV below.
6. Examples of KK-bordisms from technical origins
We consider several general classes of KK-bordisms that will play a role in the
abstract study of the KK-bordism groups. We study classical equivalences coming
from perturbations in Subsection 6.2 as well as homotopies; in Subsection 6.1 we
study homotopies of the operator and in Subsection 6.4 we study homotopies of the
left action. We also study KK-bordisms whose origins are not classical; we show
that weakly degenerate cycles produce a nullbordism in Subsection 6.3 and how
complex interpolation allows us to reduce the order of operators in Subsection 6.5.
In each of these cases, the situation at hand produces a canonical bordism.
6.1. Homotopy of the operator. A natural source of examples for KK-
bordisms comes from paths (D(t))t∈[0,1] of operators in a fixed Hilbert C∗-module.
To ensure that such paths indeed induce KK-bordisms, one can impose a technical
condition similar to differentiability. In practice, such conditions are often utilized
in commutator estimates for the derivative in the t-direction in the spirit of Kaad-
Lesch [95] and later extended in [51]. We first state the general technical result,
after which we indicate how differentiability enters the game.
Theorem 6.1. Let E and W be Hilbert C∗-modules and i : W →E an A-
locally compact dense adjointable inclusion. Assume that D = (D(t))t∈[0,1] is a
family of regular, self-adjointable operators satisfying
(1) D(t) is constant on [0, 1/2] ∪[2/3, 1]
(2) Dom (D(t)) ⊆W for all t ∈[0, 1]
(3) A ⊆∩t∈[0,1]Lip(D(t))
(4) The operator ∂min ˆ⊠D defined on
{ξ ∈C∞
c ((0, 1), W ) : ξ(t) ∈Dom (D(t)) for all t ∈[0, 1]}
is closable. Moreover, its closure T is regular and
{ξ ∈C∞([0, 1], W ) : ξ(t) ∈Dom (D(t)) for all t ∈[0, 1]}
is a core for its adjoint.
Then (E, D(t)) is a cycle for (A, B) for all t ∈[0, 1] and
((L2[0, 1]ˆ⊠E, T), (id, χ[0,1/4]∪[3/4,1])),
defines a bordism
(E, D(0)) ∼bor (E, D(1)).
We remark that the conditions of Theorem 6.1 is a variation of the notion
of unbounded operator homotopy from [128]. We shall below in Proposition 6.3
see that an unbounded operator homotopy satisfying an additional differentiability
assumption induces a family as in Theorem 6.1. Due to the technical nature of
condition 4) it is unclear if all unbounded operator homotopies induces families as
in Theorem 6.1.
Proof. We first prove that (E, D(t)) is a cycle for (A, B) for all t ∈[0, 1].
For any a ∈A, item 2) ensures that the operator (i ± D(t))−1 on E factors over
i : W →E which by assumption ensures that D(t) has A-locally compact resolvent.

Weakly degenerate cycles play an important role in connecting unbounded KK-
theory and geometric K-homology. Below in Subsection 7.1, we shall see how all
the constituents of the Baum-Douglas relation defining geometric K-homology fit
together with KK-bordisms and in particular in Corollary 7.7 we see how Theo-
rem 6.12 relates vector bundle modification to KK-bordisms. Let us turn to Kaad’s
notion of spectrally decomposable cycles and show that they are bounded perturba-
tions of special type of weakly degenerate cycles. First, we recall what a spectrally
decomposable cycle is.
Definition 6.13 (Definition 4.1 of [97]). Let (E, D) be a cycle for (A, B). We
say that (E, D) is spectrally decomposed by P if P is an orthogonal projection on
E preserving Dom (D) and satisfying
(1) P commutes with the A-action
(2) DP = PD on Dom (D)
(3) DP and D(P −1) are positive and regular operators
(4) If (E, D) is an even cycle, the operator 2P −1 is odd.
If (E, D) admits a spectrally decomposing projection, we say that (E, D) is spec-
trally decomposable.

from Theorem 6.12.

1)) is a weakly degenerate decomposition of D+iγ(2P −1) and the theorem follows
from Corollary 6.5 and Theorem 6.12.
□
Corollary 6.15. Let (E, D) be a cycle for (A, B) with D is invertible. Assume
that there is a real valued odd function χ ∈C∞(R) with χ(x) = 1 for all x > 0
belonging to the spectrum of D, such that χ(D) commutes with A. Then (E, D) is
spectrally decomposed by P := (χ(D) + 1)/2 and (E, D) is nullbordant.

7. Examples of KK-bordisms from index theory and noncommutative
geometry
In this section we will consider geometric examples of KK-bordisms, building
on the constructions from Section 6. The purpose of these examples is to exemplify
the notion of KK-bordism as well as providing explicit KK-bordisms that are of
interest to applications in secondary invariants. Similarly to the previous section, in
each of the cases the KK-bordisms arise canonically from the underlying geometry.
7.1. Dirac operators on C∗-bundles. A rather basic construction of KK-
bordisms stem from the example considered in Subsection 3.2 of a Dirac operator
twisted by a hermitean B-bundle. We refer the reader to Subsection 3.2 for ref-
erences, yet note that KK-bordisms was in this context only studied in [51]. We
follow the notation of Subsection 3.2. If W is a complete Riemannian manifold with
boundary, we say that W is of product type near the boundary if the metric gW on
W in a uniform collared neighborhood can be written as gW = dt2 + g∂W where t
denotes the unit normal variable to ∂W and by uniform collared neighborhood we
mean the subset of W defined from 0 ≤t ≤ϵ for some ϵ > 0. Similarly, we define


## Page 66

boundary.

Another instance of a KK-bordism with geometric origins come from Baum-
Douglas’ notion of vector bundle modification [12, 13, 16, 81]. This was discussed
in [51, Subsection 4.2].
In fact, the notion of weakly degenerate cycles in 6.3
is motivated by vector bundle modification and its appearance in the geometric
structure group [57]. The analytic details originate in work of Higson-Roe on η-
invariants [81].
We first consider the case of vector bundle modification of oriented manifolds by
oriented vector bundles. The spinc-case is discussed below in Remark 7.8. Oriented
vector bundle modification was studied in detail in [70]. Assume that W is an
oriented manifold with boundary and V →W is an oriented vector bundle of even
rank equipped with a Riemannian structure. For simplicity, we assume that the
rank of V is constant. Let R →W denote the trivial real line bundle. At the level

i.e. the total space of the sphere bundle πV : S(V ⊕R) →W.

7.3. Descent and assembly of equivariant cycles. The theory of KK-
bordisms from Section 5 extend to the G-equivariant setting, providing an extension
to the unbounded model of the results of [11, 102]. We follow the notations and
conventions of Subsection 3.3, recall in particular Definition 3.11 of a G-equivariant
chain. We fix a second countable locally compact group G throughout the subsec-
tion.
Definition 7.11. Assume that A and B are G −C∗-algebras and A ⊆A is
a G-invariant dense ∗-subalgebra. A G-equivariant symmetric (A, B)-chain with
boundary is an (A, B)-symmetric chain with boundary X = (Z, Θ, Y) where
(1) The boundary cycle Y = (E, D) is a G-equivariant (A, B)-cycle;
(2) The interior chain Z = (N , T) is a G-equivariant symmetric (A, B)-chain;
(3) The boundary data Θ = (θ, p) satisfies that
• p is a G-invariant projection in End∗
B(N );
• θ : pN →L2[0, 1]ˆ⊠E is a G-equivariant isomorphism.
If additionally X is a bordism, we say that X is a G-equivariant bordism.
The results of Section 5 extends ad verbatim to the G-equivariant setting. If
two G-equivariant (A, B)-cycles Y1 and Y2 admit a G-equivariant KK-bordism X
over (A, B), with ∂X = Y1 + (−Y2) we say that Y1 and Y2 are G-equivariantly
bordant and write
Y1 ∼bor,G Y2.
We define the G-equviariant KK-bordism group as
ΩG
∗(A, B) := {isomorphism classes of G-equivariant (A, B))-cycles} / ∼bor,G .
Many of the constructions above, such as those in Section 6, extend to the
equivariant setting. Let us discuss weak degeneracy in more detail. We follow the
setup of Subsection 6.3.
Definition 7.12. Let (E, D) be a G-equivariant (A, B)-chain. An equivariant
weakly degenerate decomposition of (E, D) is pair of regular operators (D0, S) such
that D = D0 + S, implicitly it thus holds that Dom (D) = Dom (D0) ∩Dom (S),
and additionally that
(1) S is G-invariant, self-adjoint and admits a bounded inverse, Dom (S) is
preserved by A and A commutes with S on Dom (S).
(2) There is a common G-invariant core for X ⊆Dom (S) ∩Dom (D0) with
SX0 ⊆Dom (D0), D0X0 ⊆Dom (S) and
SD0 + D0S = 0
on
X0.
(3) A ⊆Lip(D0).
A chain admitting an equivariant weakly degenerate decomposition is called equi-
variant weakly degenerate.

The operator Dsh is a symmetric and regular operator fitting into a bordism

(E, D) ∼bor,G 0.

7.7. Continuous trace algebras and Fell algebras. In Subsection 3.8 and
3.9 we saw constructions of cycles on continuous trace algebras and more generally
on Fell algebras. In the example above, we have seen ample examples of results
of the form “if a cycle defined on a manifold extends to a half-closed chain on a
manifold with boundary, it is KK-nullbordant”. The same type of results can be
formulated for continuous trace algebras and Fell algebras. But instead of beating
a dead horse, we contain ourselves to giving a lofty general principle and exemplify
this in a special case.
The general principle can in lofty terms be stated for a ∗-algebra AX (say
a dense ∗-subalgebra of a continuous trace algebra or a Fell algebra), and some
(AX, C)-cycle (L2(M, E), DE) for a complete Riemannian manifold M and a Dirac


## Page 77


7. EXAMPLES OF KK-BORDISMS FROM INDEX THEORY AND NCG
77
type operator on the Clifford bundle E →M. As seen in the Subsections 7.1 and
7.4, if M is bound by a complete Riemannian manifold with boundary W to which
E extends to EW →W, and there is a ∗-algebra AW on which the algebra Lip(W)
of uniformly Lipschitz functions on W acts as multipliers and there is an (AW , C)-
chain (L2(W, EW ), DEW ) which is i) half-closed for elements of AW vanishing on
the boundary, and ii) compatible with the AX-action near the boundary for a
∗-homomorphism AX →AW then (L2(M, E), DE) is KK-nullbordant as (AX, C)-
cycles via a KK-bordism with interior chain (L2(W, EW ), DEW ). We now turn to
the concrete example.
Example 7.24. As in Subsection 3.8 we consider a principal circle bundle
π : Z →X where we assume Z to be a closed manifold. We are interested in the
Schwartz algebra AX := S(R × Z) = S(R, C∞(Z)) which is a dense ∗-subalgebra
of the continuous trace algebra C(Z) ⋊R over X × S1. Assume that f : W →
Z is a Lipschitz continuous U(1)-equivariant map from a manifold with boundary,
E →W is a Clifford bundle and that DE is a Dirac operator on E. By a gluing
argument, we can assume that all structures on W are of product type near the
boundary. The pull-back f defines ∗-homomorphisms
f ∗: C(Z) ⋊R →C(W) ⋊R,
and
(f|∂W )∗: C(Z) ⋊R →C(∂W) ⋊R.
Following the general principle above, or more precisely the arguments of the Sub-
sections 7.1 and 7.4, we conclude that the (S(R × Z), C)-cycle
(f|∂W )∗(L2(∂W, E∂), D∂
E),
is KK-nullbordant via a KK-bordism with interior chain f∗(L2(W, E), DE).
7.8. Cuntz-Krieger algebras. Let us proceed with the example on the Cuntz-
Krieger algebras from Subsection 3.10. The spectral triples on Cuntz-Krieger al-
gebras from [63, 65], and more generally on Smale space C∗-algebras from [53],
provide instances of truly noncommutative objects. As such, we believe there is
milage in a truly noncommutative geometric equivalence relation among cycles.
One can for instance ask if the equivalence relations between shift groupoids stud-

7.12. Topological- and Lipschitz manifolds. We can now study the sig-
nature operator on Lipschitz manifold from Subsection 3.14 further. This operator
has been studied from the perspective of KK-theory in [86, 87, 133, 161], and
we here rephrase these results in terms of the KK-bordism groups. The advantage
of this approach, that will be utilize later for secondary invariants, is that we all
equivalences can be made explicit and geometric.
If W is a Lipschitz manifold with boundary, and we have a Lipschitz Rie-
mannian metric gW , on W, we say that gW is of product type if there is a collar
neighborhood of ∂W isometrically bi-Lipschitz homeomorphic to [0, ϵ] × ∂W for
some ϵ > 0. Similarly, we define the notion of product type for connections and
Dirac operators.

Part III
Geometry and structure in
KK-bordisms


## Page 86


8. Geometric constructions with KK-bordisms
KK-bordisms have many similarities with manifolds with boundary and clas-
sical bordisms. We will make use of some constructions for KK-bordisms whose
counterpart for manifolds with boundary are geometric in their nature. For in-
stance, the straightening-the-angle technique (see for example [137, Appendix A]
and [145]) that smoothens out low-dimensional singularities allows one to define
products of manifolds with boundaries is important in showing that the double of
a manifold is nullbordant (and in extension that the set of bordism classes of man-
ifolds forms a group). We provide analogues of these techniques in the theory of
KK-bordisms and in the later sections 9 and 10 we apply said techniques to study
structural properties of KK-bordism groups and higher Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index
theory, respectively. A number of these constructions were first considered and/or
are inspired by work of Hilsum [82, 83, 84, 85].
8.1. Straightening the angle. The classical straightening-the-angle is a tech-
nique we will need a “KK-version” of in some special cases. In classical bordism
theory, it takes the following form. The reader can find its proof in for instance
[137, Appendix A].
Theorem 8.1. Suppose that W is a topological manifold of dimension n and
M ⊂W is a closed submanifold, which is smooth, with empty boundary, and of
dimension n −2. In addition, suppose that
(1) W \ M has a differentiable structure;
(2) there exists a neighbourhood U of M in W and a homeomorphism
φ : U →M × R+ × R+
that identifies M with M × {0} × {0} and is a diffeomorphism on U \ M.
Then there is a differentiable structure on W which extends that on W \ M and
makes W a smooth manifold.
Example 8.2. Let W be a smooth manifold with boundary.
Then we can
straighten the angle on W × [0, 1] to obtain a smooth manifold ˆW which is homeo-
morphic to W × [0, 1] by a map smooth outside of ∂W × {0, 1}. In particular, the
boundary of ˆW is homeomorphic to the double 2W of W and is obtained as the
union
W × {0} ∪∂W ∂W × [0, 1] ∪∂W −W × {1}.
For example, if W = [0, 1], then W × [0, 1] is the unit cube, ˆW is up to diffeomor-
phism the closed unit disk and ∂ˆW = S1 is realized as the union of four subintervals
of S1 that ∂(W × [0, 1]) consists of.
Example 8.3. Let W1, W2 and W3 be smooth manifolds with the same bound-
ary M. Moreover, suppose that
W1 ∪M W2 = ∂Z12 and W2 ∪M W3 = ∂Z23
for some smooth manifolds with boundary Z12 and Z23. Then we can straighten
the angle on Z13 := Z12 ∪W2 Z23 to obtain a smooth manifold with boundary. It
holds that
∂Z13 = W1 ∪M W3


## Page 87


8. GEOMETRIC CONSTRUCTIONS WITH KK-BORDISMS
87
Figure 4. Straightening the angles on the unit square.
is a boundary. In other words, an explicit bounding manifold can be obtained from
Z12 and Z23. It is at first not smooth, but can be “straightened”. This explicitly
realized transitivity property will be relevant in the upcoming work [52] building
a relative homology theory where relative cycles are formed by the relations of the
theory. In our case the relations come from KK-bordisms and we will need similar
properties.
8.2. The double of a bordism. In the same way that we can double a
manifold (see Example 8.2) and obtain a boundary, one can do the same for a
KK-bordism. We will now double a KK-bordism and realize it as the boundary
of the product with the interval [0, 1] after “straightening the angle”. Recall the
suspension of a chain by the unit interval from Definition 3.1 and its properties
given in Proposition 3.2.
The suspension operation does not map a bordism to a bordism. There are two
obvious issues. Firstly, the suspension of a boundary is not a cycle as is seen clas-
sically in Figure 5. This issue is due to the fact that the boundary of a suspension
changes, and the KK-theoretic suspension should change the boundary accord-
ingly. Secondly, the suspension operation destroys the structure of the boundary
data Θ that geometrically corresponds to a collar neighborhood. The last issue
is of a more technical nature and will be fixed by “straightening the angle” at a
KK-theoretical level.
Define the piecewise smooth domain Ω0 := [1, 2] × [0, 1] ⊆R2. We will fix
a choice of a piecewise smooth compact convex domain Ω⊆[1, 2] × [0, 1] ⊆R2
satisfying
Ω1 [1, 4/3] × [0, 1] ⊆Ω;
Ω2 [5/3, 2] × [0, 1] ∩Ω= ∅; and,
Ω3 (−∞, 1] × [0, 1] ∪Ωis a domain with smooth boundary.

and X1#YX2 and X2#YX3 are nullbordant then via a canonical bordism, the cycle
X1#YX3 is also nullbordant.

8.4. Gluing on an infinite cylinder. A KK-bordism should be thought
of as a noncommutative analogue of a manifold with boundary whose geometric
structures near the boundary are of product type.
As such, we should be able
to glue on an infinite cylinder onto the boundary component. This idea is found
already in the work of Hilsum [82]. Recall the suspension of a chain by the half
interval [0, ∞) from Definition 3.3 and its properties given in Proposition 3.4.
Proposition 8.11. If Y = (E, D) is a closed (A, B)-cycle, then Ψ(0,∞)(Y) is
a well defined half-closed (C∞
c ((0, ∞), A), B)-chain and the data Θ = (id, χ[0,1] ˆ⊠1)
makes Ψ(0,∞)(Y) into a symmetric chain with boundary.
We remark that Ψ(0,∞)(Y) is never closed unless Y = 0. We omit the proof as
it follows directly from Proposition 3.4.
Assume that X = (X◦, Θ, X∂) is a symmetric chain with boundary for (A, B).
We define a half-closed (C∞
c ((0, ∞), A), B)-chain X(0,∞) by setting
X◦
(0,∞) := X#X∂Ψ(0,∞)(X∂).
We say that X◦
(0,∞) is X with an infinite cylinder glued on. For notational purposes,
we write X(0,∞) = (N(0,∞), T(0,∞)). Note that as Hilbert C∗-modules, N(0,∞) =
N ⊕L2[1, ∞)ˆ⊠M for X∂= (M , S).

defines a half-closed (C∞

(1) T(0,∞) is self-adjoint.
