For the purpose of constructions, it is sometimes convenient to work with a
different type of KK-bordisms, KK-bordisms with infinite cylindrical ends. This
notion should be viewed in the same way as one attaches infinite cylinders to the
boundary of a manifold with boundary; this procedure does not alter the homeo-
morphism type of the interior but can rather be viewed as a change of metric to a
complete metric on the interior. We formalize the notion of a bordism with infinite
cylindrical ends in the following definition.
Definition 8.15. A symmetric chain with cylindrical ends is a collection X =
(Z, Θ, Y) as in Definition 5.1 par the following conditions:
(1) The operator T is self-adjoint
(2) The boundary data Θ = (θ, p) satisfies that
• p is a projection in End∗
B(N ) that commutes with the A-action (p is
even in the graded situation);
• θ : pN →L2[0, ∞)ˆ⊠E is an isomorphism.
These objects are compatible in the following sense described using the representa-
tion
b : C0([0, ∞), A) →End∗
B(N ),
b(ϕ) := ϕ(0)(1 −p) + pθ−1ϕθ.
We require that the following conditions to hold:
a) For ϕ ∈C∞
b (0, ∞) with ϕ = 0 near 0,
b(ϕ)Dom T ∗⊆Dom T
and
T ∗b(ϕ) = Tb(ϕ)
on

b (0, ∞) with ϕ = 0 near 0
ϕDom Ψ(0,∞)(D) = θb(ϕ)Dom T
and

A symmetric chain with cylindrical ends X is said to be a bordism with cylin-

this case we say that the (A, B)-cycle ∂X := Y is the boundary of X and that the

Proposition 8.16. Let X = (Z, Θ, Y) be an (A, B)-bordism with cylindrical

The reader should interpret the notion of weak isomorphism of KK-bordisms
identifying KK-bordisms that are isomorphic “up to a choice of boundary condi-
tion”. For all intents and purposes, weakly isomorphic KK-bordisms are indis-
tinguishable because the “boundary conditions” are auxiliary. It is clear from the

9. Properties of the KK-bordism group
In this section we will utilize the KK-bordisms considered in Sections 6 and 8 to
deduce structural results about the KK-bordism groups. We conclude invariance
under functional calculus, Morita invariance under finitely generated projective
modules as well as under complex interpolation. We continue by discussing Hilsum’s
work [85] on the bounded transform and a variety of technical simplifications.
9.1. Invariance properties. We start by summarizing some immediate func-
toriality properties of the KK-bordism group in a theorem.
Theorem 9.1.
(1) Let φ : A1 →A2 be a ∗-homomorphism of ∗-algebras
which is continuous in the C∗-topologies. For any C∗-algebra B, the map-
ping
φ∗: Ω∗(A2, B) →Ω∗(A1, B),
(E, D) 7→(φE, D),
is a well defined graded group homomorphism. If φ is an inclusion, the
map φ∗is surjective if any (A1, B)-cycles is bordant to a cycle extending
to (A2, B) and the map φ∗is injective if any nullbordant (A1, B)-cycle
extending to (A2, B) is nullbordant as an (A2, B)-cycle.
(2) Let φ : B1 →B2 be a ∗-homomorphism of C∗-algebras. For any ∗-algebra
A, the mapping
φ∗: Ω∗(A, B1) →Ω∗(A, B2),
(E, D) 7→(E ⊗φ B2, D ⊗1B2),
is a well defined graded group homomorphism.

9.2. The bounded transform. An important tool in the study of the KK-
bordism groups is the bounded transform, which defines a mapping to the ordinary
KK-groups. Indeed, the KK-bordism groups find much of its relevance through
its relation to the KK-groups implemented by the bounded transform. We shall
later in this monograph see that for large classes of ∗-algebras A, the bounded
transform is an isomorphism, but in some cases it is neither injective nor surjec-
tive. A geometric analogue for the reader to keep in their mind is the mapping
ΩSpinc
∗
(X) →K∗(X) mapping a spinc-bordism class to its fundamental class; this
analogue is only partial as the KK-bordisms encode all of the Baum-Douglas rela-
tions as discussed in Remark 13.5 below.
Definition 9.6. Let A ⊆A be a dense ∗-subalgebra of the C∗-algebra A and
let B be a C∗-algebra. For an unbounded (A, B)-cycle (E, D), define the bounded
transform by
β(E, D) := (E, D(1 + D2)−1/2).
Remark 9.7. It is well known that for an unbounded (A, B)-cycle (E, D), the
bounded transform β(E, D) is an (A, B)-Kasparov cycle. This was proven by Baaj-
Julg [8]. In fact, Hilsum [85] proved that for a half-closed (A, B)-chain (E, D),
the bounded transform β(E, D) := (E, D(1 + D∗D)−1/2) is a well defined (A, B)-
Kasparov cycle.
Theorem 9.8 (Hilsum’s theorem on bordism invariance, [85]). Let A ⊆A be a
dense ∗-subalgebra of the C∗-algebra A and let B be a C∗-algebra. If an unbounded
(A, B)-cycle (E, D) is null bordant, then
[β(E, D)] = 0
in KK∗(A, B).
In particular, the bounded transform induces a well defined homomorphism
β : Ω∗(A, B) →KK∗(A, B).
For the purposes of utilizing KK-bordism in constructing relative KK-bordism
groups, let us briefly sketch the main ideas in the proof of this theorem. The proof
is based on Hilsum’s proof that β(N , T) := (N , T(1 + T ∗T)−1/2) is a well defined
Kasparov cycle for any half-closed chain (N , T). In particular, if (N , T) is the inte-
rior chain in a bordism with boundary (E, D), then β(N , T) is a (C0((0, 1]A), B)-
Kasparov cycle which with some work one can show restricts to a (C0((0, 1)A), B)-
Kasparov cycle homotopic to β ◦Ψ(E, D). The class of the Kasparov cycle β ◦
Ψ(E, D) is in turn given by the image of β(E, D) under the suspension map-
ping KK∗(A, B) →KK∗+1(C0((0, 1)A), B).
As such, β(E, D) is in the image
of 0 = KK∗+1(C0((0, 1]A), B) →KK∗(A, B) and must be 0. Unraveling the ho-
motopies in this proof is fundamental in the context of relative KK-groups and
more details will be provided in [52].
Definition 9.9. Let A be a dense ∗-subalgebra of a C∗-algebra A. We say
that A is Ω∗-admissible if the bounded transform
β : Ω∗(A, B) →KK∗(A, B),
is an isomorphism for all C∗-algebras B.
Remark 9.10. In a previous paper by the authors [51], some further properties
of the bounded transform was proven:


## Page 103


9. PROPERTIES OF THE KK-BORDISM GROUP
103
• The ∗-algebra A = C is Ω∗-admissible, i.e. β : Ω∗(C, B) →KK∗(C, B) is
an isomorphism for all C∗-algebras B.
• If M is a closed manifold and C∞(M) ⊆A ⊆Lip(M), then β : Ω∗(A, B) →
KK∗(C(M), B) is a split surjection.
We also note that the lifting result of Baaj-Julg [8] implies that if A is a count-
ably generated ∗-algebra, the bounded transform β : Ω∗(A, B) →KK∗(A, B) is a
surjection. From this surjectivity statement, and the invariance results of Subsec-
tion 9.1 we see that there is an abundance of examples where surjectivity of the
bounded transform holds. In Part IV below the reader can find ample amounts of
examples of Ω∗-admissible ∗-algebras. We shall below in Section 11 see that the
lifting method of Baaj-Julg [8] can be use to show that the bounded transform is
an isomorphism if A is a countably generated ∗-algebra. The next proposition will
be used to prove that.

In relation to the notion of weakly degenerate cycles from Subsection 6.3, we
have the following result. Recall that a Kasparov cycle (E, F) is said to be degen-
erate if for all a ∈A,
[F, a] = a(F 2 −1) = a(F ∗−F) = 0.
Proposition 9.12. Suppose that (E, D) is an (A, B)-cycle with D having
closed range and whose bounded transform β(E, D) satisfies that its operator ho-
motopic cycle (E, sign(β(D))) is degenerate, then the bounded perturbation (E, D +
iγsign(β(D))) of (E, D) is weakly degenerate. In particular, if sign of the bounded
transform of an (A, B)-cycle is degenerate, the cycle is weakly degenerate.
Proof. The result is immediate from Corollary 6.15. Alternatively, the propo-
sition follows from Proposition 6.17 and the fact that iγsign(β(D)) is a Clifford
symmetry.
□
We note that several of the results in this monograph holds also in the equi-
variant setting. Recall the terminology from the Subsections 3.3 and 7.3. For a

which is considered as a G-equivariant Kasparov cycle. We have the following result
by the same proof as in the non-equivariant case.

10. Higher Atiyah-Patodi-Singer theory for KK-bordisms
As is the case for a manifold with boundary, one can equip the operator appear-
ing in a KK-bordism with Fredholm boundary conditions (of APS-type) and obtain
an index associated with a KK-bordism. For manifolds, these boundary conditions
were first introduced by Atiyah-Patodi-Singer and our approach follows the subse-
quent developments for Dirac operators on manifolds with boundary twisted by a
C∗-bundle. The idea of studying APS-realizations of KK-bordisms was introduced
and developed by Hilsum [82]. The purpose of this section is to set Hilsum’s ideas
of APS-realizations of KK-bordisms in the foundations developed in the preceding
parts of this monograph.
The main technical issue concerns the existence of the boundary conditions, but
under a suitable technical assumption that we call very full (see Definition 10.2),
the theory can be developed just as for manifolds with boundary and a C∗-bundle.
To overcome this issue we follow the methodology of [117]. The main difference to
[117] is that there a considerable effort was needed to prove that manifolds twisted
by a C∗-bundle are very full, that we in the case of KK-bordisms deem to be a
property forced only by assumption. We restrict to the case of unital cycles (see
Definition 2.1); this restriction is not that important for some of the preliminary
results but will make a difference for the index theoretical results.
Throughout this section we let B denote a unital C∗-algebra and consider KK-
bordisms and cycles for (C, B).
10.1. Spectral sections of cycles. Let Y = (E, D) be a cycle for (C, B).
Definition 10.1. Let Y = (E, D) be a cycle for (C, B).
• A spectral cut for Y is an operator of the form χ(D) ∈End∗
B(E) where
χ ∈C∞(R, [0, 1]) satisfies that χ(t) = 0 for t ≪0 and χ(t) = 1 for t ≫0.
• A spectral section for an odd cycle Y is a projection P ∈End∗
B(E) such
that χ1(D)E ⊆PE ⊆χ2(D)E for two spectral cuts χ1(D) and χ2(D).
If Y is an even cycle, a spectral section is a projection P ∈End∗
B(E)
satisfying the condition for odd cycles and additionally P −1/2 is an odd
operator.
The main technical problem in this subsection is to find a sufficient condition
for the existence of spectral sections. The case of a general cycle is quite similar to
the situation of a Dirac operator on a closed manifold twisted by a C∗-bundle, see

The following result shows that if there exists a spectral section, there is an
abundance of spectral sections. The proof goes as in [117, Section 2.5], for more
details, see also [159].
Lemma 10.6. Let Y = (E, D) be a cycle admitting a spectral section.
(1) Let χ1(D) be a spectral cut. Then there is a spectral cut χ2(D) such that
χ1χ2 = χ1 and a spectral section P such that χ1(D)E ⊆PE ⊆χ2(D)E.
(2) Let P1 and P2 be spectral sections. There there exists spectral sections Q1
and Q2 such that for j = 1, 2
PjQ1 = Q1Pj = Pj
and
PjQ2 = Q2Pj = Q2.
Spectral sections in fact always arises as positive spectral projections of certain
perturbations of D.

Definition 10.7. Let Y = (E, D) be a cycle. A trivializing operator for Y is

• for some R > 0, ψ(D)A = Aψ(D) = 0 for all bounded ψ ∈C∞(R) with

• D + A is invertible.

Corollary 10.9. Assume that (E, D) is a unital very full (A, B)-cycle. Then
the following are equivalent:
(1) There exists a bounded selfadjoint operator A ∈End∗
B(E), which can be
taken odd if (E, D) is even, such that D + A is invertible.
(2) indB(D) = 0
(3) (E, D) is null-bordant.
Proof. It follows from [51, Theorem 3.7] that (2) and (3) are equivalent.
Clearly, (1) implies (2). That (2) implies (1) follows from Theorem 10.3 and Propo-

10.2. Atiyah-Patodi-Singer theory for KK-bordisms. Let X be a bor-
dism for (C, B). Recall that the boundary X∂= (M , S) of X is a cycle for (C, B).
Definition 10.10. Let X be a bordism for (C, B) with boundary X∂.
• We say that X admits a spectral section if X∂admits a spectral section.
In this case, a spectral section for X will refer to a spectral section for X∂
and a trivializing operator for X will refer to a trivializing operator for
X∂.
• We say that X is very full if X∂is very full.
It follows from bordism invariance of the index that indB(X∂) = 0. By Corol-
lary 10.9, a very full (C, B)-bordism X admits a spectral section.
We shall now give two index theoretically equivalent ways of constructing a B-
Fredholm operator from a bordism X and a choice of trivializing operator (assuming
it exists). The two approaches are to glue on an infinite cylinder (as in Subsection
8.4) or to equip the interior operator in the bordism with APS-boundary conditions.
Fix a bordism X and a trivializing operator A.

Theorem 10.16. Let X be a bordism with a trivializing operator A. Then the

Definition 10.17. Let X be a (C, B)-bordism. Define its very full modification

Theorem 10.18. Let X be a (C, B)-bordism.
Its very full modification Xvf
satisfies the following:
(1) ∂Xvf = ∂X + YS1,B and Xvf is very full.

The first item of Theorem 10.18, together with Theorem 10.3 (see page 105)
and Proposition 10.8 (see page 107), shows that the very full modification of a
bordism always admits a trivializing operator.
The second item of Theorem 10.18 shows that the APS-index of a bordism is
independent of very full modification. The reader should take this statement with
a grain of salt, since the APS-index still depends on trivializing operators and there
are more trivializing operators for the very full modification. However, it allows us
to make the following definition.
Definition 10.19. Let X be a (C, B)-bordism with trivializing operator A, we
define
indAPS(X, A) := indB(TAPS(A)) ≡indB(T∞(A)).
More generally, if X is a (C, B)-bordism and A is a trivializing operator for the very
full modification, we set
indAPS(X, A) := indAPS(Xvf, A).

Example 10.20 (APS-theory for proper actions). Let us study some further
details for the chains studied in Example 3.2 and 3.4. Consider a (second countable)
locally compact group G acting properly and cocompactly by smooth isometries on
a Riemannian manifold with boundary W. Consider a unital G−C∗-algebra B and
a G-equivariant Clifford B-bundle EB →W. Pick a complete Dirac operator /DE.
By the arguments of Example 7.4, (L2(W, EB), DE,min) fits into a G-equivariant
(C, B)-bordism. Applying assembly, we arrive at a (C, B ⋊G)-bordism that we
by an abuse of notation write as µ(L2(W, EB), DE,min). The constructions above
therefore gives meaning to APS-index theory for G-equivariant Clifford B-bundle
EB →W over a proper cocompact manifold with boundary W, and for a trivializing
operator A we arrive at a higher APS-index
indAPS(µ(L2(W, EB), DE,min), A) ∈K∗(B ⋊G).
APS-index theory for proper cocompact actions have been studied in [71, 90] for
invertible boundary operators.
We remark that it is an open problem to show that in this context, the null-
bordant cycle ∂µ(L2(W, EB), DE,min) is very full. The direct method of proof from
[117], see in particular [117, page 358], shows that ∂µ(L2(W, EB), DE,min) is very
full as soon as there exists a collection (ek)k∈N ⊆µ(L2(∂W, E∂
B)) ⊗B⋊G M(B ⋊G)
with ⟨ek, ej⟩= δjk1 and χ(µ(D∂
E))ek = ek for a spectral cut.
Example 10.21 (APS-theory for Lipschitz manifolds with boundary). We con-
tinue to study Lipschitz manifolds as in Subsection 3.14 and 7.12. Consider a unital
C∗-algebra B and a hermitean B-bundle EB →W with connection over a compact
Riemannian Lipschitz manifold with boundary W. Form the twisted signature op-
erator Dsign,E on L2(W; ∧∗⊗EB) as in Subsection 3.14.The constructions above
therefore gives meaning to APS-index theory for twisted signature operators on
Lipschitz manifolds with boundary W, and for a trivializing operator A we arrive
at a higher APS-index
indAPS((L2(W; ∧∗⊗EB), Dsign,E), A) ∈K∗(B).
APS-index theory for Lipschits manifolds have been studied in [83] for the scalar
case B = C where there is no need for trivializing operators.
10.3. Gluing, relative indices and spectral flows. We saw how to define
the APS-index of a bordism equipped with a trivializing operator in the last sub-
section. It is at this stage unclear how the APS-index depends on the trivializing
operator. The reader can rest easy, its dependence on the trivializing operator will
behave in the same way as it does on a manifold.
We shall make this analogy
mathematically specific in this subsection by proving gluing formulas and spectral
flow formulas for the APS-index. The results of this subsection are well known (see
e.g. [117]) in the case of manifolds with boundary, and most of the proofs extend
ad verbatim from that case.
Theorem 10.22 (Gluing formula for the APS-index). Let X1 and X2 be (C, B)-
bordisms with
∂X1 = Y1 + (−Y2)
and
X2 = Y2 + (−Y3)

A3 are trivializing operators for the very full modifications of Y1, Y2 and Y3 the
equality (41) still holds.

Theorem 10.26 (APS-index of a cylinder). Let Y be a (C, B)-bordism admit-

that
indAPS(Ψ(Y), −A ⊕A′) = sf(Y; A, A′),

direct proof in the case that Y is odd.

Theorem 10.27 (Spectral flow formula for the APS-index). Let X be a (C, B)-
bordism admitting a trivializing operator. For two trivializing operators A and A′
of X, it holds that
indAPS(X, A) −indAPS(X, A′) = sf(∂X; A, A′).
Proof. Again using Bunke’s proof of his relative index theorem [29] (for the
operators T#(−T), T∞(A), −T∞(−A′), and Ψ(D)∞(A ⊕−A′)) we have that
indAPS(X, A) −indAPS(X, A′) = indB(Ψ(D)∞(A ⊕−A′)).
We are here using that X# −X is a nullbordant closed cycle whose index therefore
vanishes. By Theorem 10.16, the index of Ψ(D)∞(A ⊕−A′) is the APS-index of a
cylinder as in Theorem 10.26.
□
Let us introduce yet another description of the APS-index of a bordism, it will
be useful for relative constructions. Recall the notion of the transgression bordism
from Definition 6.6 and the Shubin bordism from Theorem 6.12.
Definition 10.28. Assume that Y = (E, D) is a (C, B)-cycle with trivializing
operator A. Then, by definition, (E, D + A) is weakly degenerate (for the weakly
degenerate decomposition D0 = 0 and S = D + A) and we define the transgressed
Shubin bordism
Sh(E, D, A) := T (E, D, A)#(E,D+A)Sh(E, D + A).
Lemma 10.29. Assume that Y is a (C, B)-cycle with trivializing operator A.
Then the transgressed Shubin bordism Sh(Y, A) is a well defined (C, B)-bordism
with boundary Y. Moreover, we have that
indAPS(Sh(Y, A), A) = 0.
Proof. The collection Sh(Y, A) is a bordism since it is a gluing of KK-
bordisms. That indAPS(Sh(Y, A), A) = 0 follows from the fact that the associated
operator has vanishing kernel. Indeed, the kernel is found from solving an explicit
operator valued ODE on R and any homogeneous solution f thereof must fulfil
f(t0) ∈imχ(0,∞)(D + A) ∩imχ(−∞,0)(D + A) = 0,
for some t0 ≫0. Therefore f = 0.
□
From Lemma 10.29, and the gluing formula for APS-indices (Theorem 10.22)
we deduce the following theorem.

Theorem 10.30 (APS-index from a Shubin suspension). Let X be a (C, B)-
bordism with a trivializing operator A. Then X#∂XSh(∂X, A) is a (C, B)-cycle with
index computed from
indB(X#∂XSh(∂X, A)) = indAPS(X, A).
In other words, the APS-index of (X, A) can be computed either from equipping
the interior cycle with APS boundary conditions TAPS(A), or attaching an infinite
cylinder and perturbing by A arriving at T∞(A) or finally by attaching an infinite
cone (by means of a linearly growing perturbation defined from A).
The last statement follows from the first and Theorem 10.16.
10.4. Cayley- and Higson transforms. The main usage of APS index the-
ory in this monograph will be when constructing secondary invariants for various
relative constructions using KK-bordisms. We will now discuss a construction re-
lating to correcting for the dependence on the choice of trivializing operator. The
two constructions, the Cayley transform of odd cycles and the Higson transform of
even cycles, can be seen as implementing an isomorphism Ω∗(C, B) →K∗(B). We
consider only A = C and B unital in this subsection. We tacitly assume that all cy-
cles are unital, in which case a (C, B)-cycle is a pair (E, D) where D is self-adjoint,
regular with compact resolvent (and odd if E is graded).

## Page 119


Part IV
Isomorphism results for
KK-bordism groups


## Page 120


In this part of the monograph we study the qualitative properties of the bounded
transform
β : Ω∗(A, B) →KK∗(A, B).
Recall the bounded transform discussed in Subsection 9.2. The bounded transform
is well defined by work of Hilsum [85]. We are interested in finding a generically
applicable sufficient condition for β to be an isomorphism. For countably generated
A, surjectivity of β follows from Baaj-Julg’s lifting theorem [8]. Injectivity of β is
essentially a surjectivity statement at the level of equivalences of cycles, and Baaj-
Julg’s lifting argument was applied in this context independently by Kaad [97] and
Mesland-van Dungen [128] to homotopy classes of unbounded Kasparov cycles. We
follow Kaad’s argument [97] to lift operator homotopies to KK-bordisms proving
β to be an isomorphism for countably generated A. Using the technology of Part I
and III, we arrive at a plethora of geometric examples when β is an isomorphism.
To complement this picture, we show that when A is an amenable Banach algebra,
then injectivity and surjectivity of β fails under additional mild assumptions on A.
11. The bounded transform for countably generated A
The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.
Theorem 11.1. Let A be a countably generated ∗-algebra with C∗-closure A.
For any C∗-algebra B, the bounded transform
β : Ω∗(A, B) →KK∗(A, B),
is an isomorphism.
With minor modifications, our proof of Theorem 11.1 extends to show that the
equivariant bounded transform
β : ΩG
∗(A, B) →KKG
∗(A, B),
is an isomorphism as soon as A is countably generated and G is either compact or
discrete and acting trivially on B. We prove the theorem by a sequence of lemmas.

12. Homotopy relations and universal cycles
We can now relate the KK-bordism group to the homotopy constructions
of Kaad [97] and Mesland-van Dungen [128]. We do again emphasize how the
KK-bordism group and the homotopy groups are constructed with different aims.
The KK-bordism group comes with an abundance of geometric exemples but also
with additional technicalities.
The homotopy groups has a conceptual elegance
but can prove unwieldely in geometric examples, for instance in realizing geomet-
ric bordisms. Mesland-van Dungen [128] considers a universal homotopy group
UKK∗(A, B) which by definition is independent of the dense subalgebra, so we
first formulate the result in terms of Kaad’s unbounded KK-group UKtop(A, B).
The group UKtop(A, B) is defined as the set of equivalence classes of (A, B)-cycles
under the equivalence relation generated by degenerate equivalence and unbounded
operator homotopy, for details see [97, Section 2]. For clarity, let us also mention
that there are other approaches to refining KK-theory that are nicely overviewed
in [46].

The theorem is immediate from Theorem 9.8 and 11.1, and [97, Theorem 7.1].
A new approach to unbounded KK-theory was found [128] that does not
require fixing a dense subalgebra A ⊆A.
In [128], universal unbounded KK-
cycles for a pair of C∗-algebras (A, B) were introduced and the corresponding set
of homotopy classes form an abelian group UKK∗(A, B) naturally isomorphic to
KK∗(A, B) via the bounded transform. We will in future work [52] make use of the
universal picture of unbounded KK-theory below to prove independence of dense
subalgebra A ⊆A in certain relative constructions.
Definition 12.2. Let A and B be two C∗-algebras.
• A universal A−B-chain is a triple (E, D, i) where i : A →A is an inclusion
of C∗-algebras and (E, D) is an (A, B)-chain for some dense A ⊆A.
• A universal A −B-chain is said to be symmetric, half-closed or closed if
(E, D) is so. A closed universal chain is called a universal cycle.
• A universal A −B-bordism consists of (X, i) where i : A →A is an
inclusion of C∗-algebras and X = (X◦, Θ, X∂) is an (A, B)-bordism for
some dense A ⊆A. In this case, the universal A −B-cycle X∂is said to
be nullbordant which is written as
X∂∼un−bor 0.
• An isomorphism of two universal A−B-chains (E1, D1, i1) and (E2, D2, i2)
is a unitary isomorphism U : E1
∼
−→E2 such that D1 = U ∗D2U and such
that the induced A-actions on E1 and E2 coincide under U.
We write Zun
∗(A, B) for the set of isomorphism classes of universal A −B-cycles.
Remark 12.3. We note that a universal A −B-cycle is a minor variation of
the unbounded A −B-cycles in [128], the latter consists of triples (π, E, D) where
π : A →Lip0(D).
We include the inclusion i in the definition to simplify the
notation in limiting arguments.
It is clear that Zun
∗(A, B) forms an abelian semigroup under direct sum
(E1, D1, i1) + (E2, D2, i2) := (E1 ⊕E2, D1 ⊕D2, i1 ⊕i2),
where i1 ⊕i2 : A →A1 ⊕A2 denotes a 7→i1(a) ⊕i2(a). If we have an equality of
universal cycles
(E1, D1, i1) + (−(E2, D2, i2)) = X∂,
for a universal A −B-bordism (X, i) we write (E1, D1, i1) ∼un−bor (E2, D2, i2) and
say that the two cycles are universally bordant.

13. Examples when the bounded transform is an isomorphism
While Theorem 11.1 is only stated for countably generated ∗-algebras, we can
readily generate more examples using the rigidity properties of Part I and III. We
summarize this procedure in the following theorem.
Theorem 13.1. Let A be a ∗-algebra with C∗-closure A. Assume that there
exists a countably generated subalgebra A0 ⊆A, a number α0 > 1 such that A ⊆
A(α0) or for some α1 ∈(0, 1] there is a Banach ∗-algebra A1 ⊆A(α0) such that
A ⊆[A1, A]α1. Then A is Ω∗-admissible, i.e. for any C∗-algebra B, the bounded
transform
β : Ω∗(A, B) →KK∗(A, B),
is an isomorphism.
Theorem 13.1 follows directly from combining Theorem 9.2 and 9.3 with The-
orem 11.1. While the assumptions of Theorem 13.1 might seem technical at first,
we shall below see numerous examples where the conditions naturally appears and
are easy to verify.
13.1. Manifolds and closed subsets of the Hilbert cube. In this section
we will give examples of commutative ∗-algebras for which the bounded transform
is an isomorphism. We will make use of the the Hilbert cube
□:= [−1, 1]N.
By Tychonoff’s theorem, the Hilbert cube is compact. We can consider □as a
separable metric space by embedding □into the unit ball of ℓ2(N, R) as the subset
Q
n∈N[−1/(n + 1), 1/(n + 1)] of sequences (xn)n∈N such that |xn| ≤1/(n + 1). Any
compact, separable, metric space embeds isometrically into □.
For the sake of
examples, we also take a Cantor set C.

14. Exactness and homotopy invariance in KK-bordism groups
Two important tools to compute KK-groups relies on their exactness and ho-
motopy invariance. In light of Theorem 13.1, there is an abundance of Ω∗-admissible
∗-algebras, so we can transfer results for KK-groups to KK-bordism groups. We
note that the proofs of the results in this section are more or less trivial, as they
piggyback on corresponding results in KK-theory, but we include them for com-
pleteness and hope that the results can be appreciated as having rather deep impli-
cations in Hilsum’s theory of KK-bordisms (see for instance Remark 14.4 below).
Theorem 14.1. Let A be an Ω∗-admissible ∗-algebra and

15. Examples when the bounded transform is not an isomorphism
We will prove that if A is an amenable Banach ∗-algebra that includes densely
and continuously in a C∗-algebra A then Ω∗(A, C) is ”small” in a precise sense.
This yields examples of dense subalgebras for which Ω∗(A, C) ≇KK∗(A, C). The
same argument applies to Kaad’s group UKtop(A, C) from [97].
For a Banach bimodule X over a Banach algebra A we denote by HH1(A, X)
the first Hochschild cohomology group of A with coefficients in X. We recall that
a bimodule derivation δ : A →X is a continuous linear map
δ : A →X,
∀a, b ∈A
δ(ab) = aδ(b) + δ(a)b.
The space of all such derivations is denoted Z1(A, X). A bimodule derivation δ is
called inner if there exists x ∈X such that δ(a) = δx(a) := ax −xa. The space
of inner derivations is denoted B1(A, X) and is clearly a subspace of Z1(A, X).
The first Hochschild cohomology group of A with coefficients in X then admits the
description
HH1(A, X) := Z1(A, X)
B1(A, X).
For a Banach bimodule X, the dual module is the continuous dual space X∗pro-
vided with the bimodule structure
a · φ · b(x) := φ(bxa),
a, b ∈A,
x ∈X.


## Page 132


132
We call Banach bimodule Y a dual Banach bimodule if Y = X∗for some Banach
bimodule X.
Definition 15.1. A Banach algebra A is amenable if HH1(A, X∗) = 0 for all
Banach bimodules X. We say that A is B(H)-amenable if HH1(A, B(H)) = 0.
Note that since B(H) = L1(H)∗, B(H) is a dual Banach bimodule. Therefore
amenability implies B(H)-amenability.
Lemma 15.2. Let A a B(H)-amenable Banach algebra and (A, H, D) an un-
bounded (A, C)-cycle. Then there exists R ∈B(H) such that [D −R, a] = 0 for all
a ∈A. If H is graded and the operator D is odd, then R can be chosen to be odd.
Proof. It follows from Proposition 4.1 that δD(a) := [D, a] defines a bimodule
derivation δD : A →B(H). Since A is B(H)-amenable, there exists R ∈B(H) such
that δD(a) = δR(a) = [R, a]. In case (A, H, D) is graded, since [D, a] is an odd
operator, it follows that [R, a] is an odd operator and thus the even part of R
commutes with A. We can thus without loss of generality replace R by its odd
part. The result now follows.
□
Proposition 15.3. Let A be an B(H)-amenable Banach algebra, (A, H, D) an
unbounded (A, C)-cycle and R be as in Lemma 15.2. Then the A representation on
H restricts to ker(D −R) and there it acts by compact operators. We have
[(A, H, D)] = [(A, ker(D −R), 0)],
in Ω∗(A, C).
Proof. Since R is bounded (A, H, D) is bordant to (A, H, D −R). Denote by
ι : ker(D −R) →H the inclusion. The cycle
(44)

However, the cycle (44) is degenerate, as its operator is invertible and commutes
with A. Hence it is null-bordant and
[(A, H, D −R)] −[(A, ker(D −R), 0)] = 0,
in Ω∗(A, C) as desired.
□
Theorem 15.4. Let A be a B(H)-amenable Banach algebra that includes densely
and continuously in A. Then Ω1(A, C) = 0 and Ω0(A, C) is generated by KK-
bordism classes of ∗-homomorphisms π : A →K(H)
Proof. By Proposition 15.3 any element in Ω∗(A, C) can be represented by
a cycle (A, H, 0) (in particular A acts by compact operators). Therefore Ω0(A, C)
consists of bordism classes of compact A representations and Ω1(A, C) = 0.
□
Remark 15.5. Theorem 15.4 is an instance of the principle that if A ⊆A is
“too big”, then there are to few (A, B)-cycles to exhaust KK∗(A, B). We note
that this stands in sharp contrast to the situation for K-theory, where the opposite
phenomen occurs: if A ⊆A is “too small” then both injectivity and surjectivity of
K∗(A) →K∗(A) can fail.


## Page 133


15. EXAMPLES WHEN THE BOUNDED TRANSFORM IS NOT AN ISOMORPHISM
133
We can apply Theorem 15.4 to concrete B(H)-amenable Banach algebras so as
to conclude that Ω∗(A, C) ≇KK∗(A, C). A first class of examples arises by taking
A = A a C∗-algebra. Recall that a C∗-algebra A has the similarity property if
every homomorphism A →B(H) is similar to a ∗-homomorphism, and A has the
derivation property if every derivation δ : A →B(H) is inner. By a theorem of
Kirchberg [106] these two properties are equivalent.
Corollary 15.6. If A is a nuclear C∗-algebra or a C∗-algebra with no tracial
states, and K1(A) ̸= 0 there is no isomorphism Ω∗(A, C) ∼= KK∗(A, C).
Proof. All nuclear C∗-algebras and all traceless C∗-algebras have the simi-
larity property and hence the derivation property. Thus it follows from Theorem
15.4 that Ω1(A, C) = 0. If K1(A) = KK1(A, C) ̸= 0, there is no isomorphism
Ω∗(A, C) ∼= KK∗(A, C).
□
Clearly, the counterexamples arising from taking A to be a C∗-algebra are
somewhat artificial. A more natural class of counterexamples arises from convolu-
tion algebras of locally compact groups. Recall that for a locally compact group G,
L1(G) is amenable if and only if G is amenable by a theorem of Johnson [93].
Corollary 15.7. If G is an amenable locally compact group for which K1(C∗(G)) ̸=
0, there is no isomorphism Ω∗(L1(G), C) ≇KK∗(C∗(G), C).
Proof. By Johnson’s theorem [93], L1(G) is B(H)-amenable. Thus for amenable
groups we can apply Theorem 15.4 to L1(G) to deduce Ω1(L1(G), C) = 0.
□
We note that G = Z satisfies all the assumptions of Corollary 15.7. The Fourier
transform realizes ℓ1(Z) as a ∗-subalgebra of C(S1) – the Wiener algebra. Let Z[S1]
denote the free group generated on S1 viewed as a discrete set. It is readily deduced
from Theorem 15.4 that there is a surjection
Z[S1] →Ω∗(ℓ1(Z), C).
The mapping sends a point t to the even spectral triple (Ct, 0) where Ct := C as an
evenly graded Hilbert space with a left action of ℓ1(Z) given by the representation
of Z with character t. We note that the class of (Ct, 0) in Ω∗(ℓ1(Z) ∩C1(S1), C)
is independent of t. Indeed, for s, t ∈S1 said bordism stems from Theorem 6.19
using an interval [s0, t0] ⊆[0, 1) for e2πis0 = s and e2πit0 = t. We can even say that
Ω∗(ℓ1(Z) ∩C1(S1), C) ∼= Z for ∗= 0, 1 using Theorem 9.2 and Corollary 13.4. The
situation for ℓ1(Z) is drastically different.
Theorem 15.8. The mapping defined in the proceeding paragraph is an iso-
morphism
Z[S1] ∼= Ω∗(ℓ1(Z), C).

16. Examples when Ω∗(−, C) fails to satisfy excision
A natural question is to ask whether the KK-bordism groups can be computed
from excision. This is the case for Kasparov’s KK-groups: exact sequences arise
from semisplit extensions as shown in for instance [22, Chapter 19.5], see also
[74], and plays an important role in applications [73, 77]. In this section we will
construct an example where excision in the contravariant leg fails. Of course, if we
are in the class of ∗-algebras studied in Section 11 where the bounded transform is
an isomorphism, excision holds because it holds in KK-theory. Our construction
relies on the counterexamples to be bounded transform being an isomorphism from
Section 15 in combination with the computational virtues of Section 11.
Let A denote the Wiener algebra on S1. This algebra consists of functions a ∈
C(S1) whose Fourier expansion a = P
k∈Z akzk satisfies that P
k |ak| < ∞. In other
words, A can be defined from the property that it is isomorphic to ℓ1(Z) under the
Fourier transform. We note that A ,→C(S1) is closed under holomorphic functional
calculus and induces an isomorphism on K-theory. Nevertheless, amenability of Z
and Theorem 15.4 shows that Ω1(A, C) = 0 despite KK1(C(S1), C) ∼= Z.
Define the *-subalgebra B ⊆T of the Toeplitz algebra to consist of Toeplitz
operators with symbol in A. By construction, there is a short exact sequence of
∗-algebras
